      Since G31 and G23 are known, this implication is verified: both unknown
      transfer functions G21 and G13 are globally identifiable from exciting w1 and
      measuring w1 and w2 .



2.4     Presence of loops

      In much of the literature, it is commonly assumed that all diagonal entries of
      G (z; θ ) must be zero, meaning that loops in the network are not permitted;
      equivalently, Gii (z; θ ) = 0 for all i. In this section, we first present the
      reasoning underlying this assumption, and then explain why it does not
      apply in our framework.
         The usual argument is that the presence of a loop prevents the network
      from being generically globally identifiable. To illustrate this reasoning,
      consider the following simple example with two nodes and two transfer
      functions (for clarity, we omit the arguments (t) and (z; θ ) in the notation):

                                                       G21
                                           w1                       w2         G22
             0           0
         G=
            G21         G22
         We have the following relation between node states 1 and 2:

                                       w2 = G21 w1 + G22 w2 .                                     (2.6)

      Multiplying both sides of the equation by 2 gives

                                    2w2 = 2G21 w1 + 2G22 w2 ,                                     (2.7)


                                                                                              | 25
                                                     Presence of loops | 2.5

and substracting w2 from both sides of the equation brings

                        w2 = 2G21 w1 + (2G22 − 1)w2 .                        (2.8)

   Assume that the network is generically globally identifiable, and the
identified transfer functions are

                              G21 = f ,   G22 = g,

Then, by (2.8), we automatically also have

                           w2 = 2 f w1 + (2g − 1)w2 ,

Hence G21 = 2 f , G22 = (2g − 1) is also a solution, which contradicts the
assumption that the network is generically globally identifiable.
   However, in our framework this argument does not apply, for the fol-
lowing reason. Consider that node 2 is excited. Equations (2.6), (2.7), (2.8)
become, respectively:

                       w2 = G21 w1 + G22 w2 + r2                             (2.9)
                   ⇔ 2w2 = 2G21 w1 + 2G22 w2 + 2r2                          (2.10)
                    ⇔ w2 = 2G21 w1 + (2G22 − 1)w2 + 2r2 .                   (2.11)

In this case, the argument developed above no longer holds: if the network
is generically globally identifiable with G21 = f and G22 = g, then the
alternative choice G21 = 2 f and G22 = (2g − 1) is not a valid solution. This
is ruled out by the factor 2 in front of r2 in (2.11), together with the fact that
the entries of the matrices B and C are binary.
    For clarity of exposition, the argument in this section has been illus-
trated using a simple two-node example, where an arbitrary factor of 2 was
introduced to derive equation (2.7) from equation (2.6). Nevertheless, the
argument extends to arbitrary networks containing loops, and the choice of
the factor is not restricted to 2.




                                                                           | 26
                                       Genericity and pseudo-genericity | 2.5


2.5     Genericity and pseudo-genericity

      We will see that properties of local identifiability - which will be introduced
      in Section 4.1 - are generic (also denoted structural in the literature), or
      pseudo-generic. [DCVdW03, HGB18] define genericity on a parameterized
      model. We extend this definition to classes of functions, since we will see in
      Section 4.2 that local identifiability can be studied via the local injectivity
      of a function. Generic properties are defined similarly in other areas of
      mathematics, including topology [Bon06, EGK+ 24].

      Definition 2.3. For two sets M and N , let F ⊆ { f : M → N } be a class
      of functions. A property of f ( x ) is generic in F if, for each f ∈ F , it either
      holds:

        (i) for almost all variables, i.e. for all x except possibly those lying on a
            set of dimension lower than dim M,
       (ii) for no x.

          As an illustrative example, consider the class of polynomials, and take
      a polynomial p. The nonzeroness of p( x ) is a generic property in the poly-
      nomials: either (i) p( x ) ̸= 0 for all x except its roots, or (ii) p is the zero
      polynomial, which returns zero for all x. A handy consequence of Definition
      2.3 is the following: showing that a generic property holds for one variable
      x implies that it holds for almost all x.
          Some properties of local injectivity and local identifiability will be shown
      in Sections 5.1 and 5.2 to be only pseudo-generic, a new slightly more general
      notion that we introduce below.

      Definition 2.4. For two sets M and N , let F ⊆ { f : M → N } be a class
      of functions. A property of f ( x ) is pseudo-generic in F if, for each f ∈ F , it
      either holds:

        (i) for almost all variables, i.e. for all x except possibly those lying on a
            set of dimension lower than dim M,
       (ii) for almost no variables, i.e. only for x lying on a set of dimension lower
            than dim M.



                                                                                 | 27
                                Genericity and pseudo-genericity | 2.5

     Typical examples of pseudo-generic properties are conditions which
require that the rank of a matrix is greater than the rank of another matrix.
A disadvantage of pseudo-genericity is that we lose the ‘handy consequence’
of genericity stated above: if the property holds for one variable x, it does
not imply that it holds for almost all x: it could lie in the lower-dimensional
set of case (ii). On the other hand, an advantage of pseudo-genericity is that
it is preserved under any finite boolean function.

Lemma 2.5. Any finite boolean function of pseudo-generic properties is pseudo-
generic.


Proof. First, any boolean function can be expressed in its disjunctive normal
form, i.e. using only ∧, ∨, ¬ (respectively logic AND,OR,NOT) [Whi12]. Recall
De Morgan’s law: Let P, Q be boolean propositions, we have

                          P ∧ Q ⇔ ¬ (¬ P ∨ ¬ Q).

Therefore, any boolean function can be expressed with only ∨ and ¬, and
proving that ∨ and ¬ preserve pseudo-genericity will yield the result.
   By definition, a pseudo-generic property either holds for almost all (AA)
parameters, or for almost no (AN) parameters. If a property P holds for
almost all parameters, then it does not hold for almost no parameters, i.e.
¬ P holds for almost no parameters. The reciprocal holds as well, and we
can summarize this in the table below.
                                   P     ¬P
                                  AA     AN
                                  AN     AA
Hence, ¬ preserves pseudo-genericity. We now consider ∨, and write the
table below.
                              P      Q     P∨Q
                             AA     AA      AA
                             AA     AN      AA
                             AN     AA      AA
                             AN     AN      AN
(Note that the last row would not hold for an infinite combination of ∨.)
Thus, ∨ preserves pseudo-genericity.



                                                                        | 28
                                Genericity and pseudo-genericity | 2.5

Note 2.6. The property of Lemma 2.5 does not hold for genericity, since ¬
does not preserve genericity: P is generic ̸⇒ ¬ P is generic.

    Most properties are neither generic, nor pseudo-generic. For instance,
consider the class of linear functions F = {y = ax + b}, and the property
‘increasing’ (i.e. a > 0). Half of the linear functions are increasing, and the
other half are not. (Pseudo-)generic properties enable reasoning about the
problem in a nearly universal manner. These properties are contingent on
the problem’s structure rather than on specific instances of the problem,
except for certain pathological cases.




                                                                        | 29
                  The full excitation (resp.
                                                                          3
                      measurement) case


      W
                 HEN all nodes are excited (resp. measured), the matrix B (resp.
                 C) reduces to the identity matrix, which simplifies the problem
                 notably. In this chapter, we present key results from the literature
      that address this fully excited or fully measured setting. These works
      begin with an algebraic characterization of identifiability in terms of the
      generic rank of a network matrix, then translate this condition into graph-
      theoretic terms involving walks in the network. This reformulation enables
      the development of algorithms for excitation allocation. Inspired by these
      works, this thesis follows the same approach.



3.1     Characterization with mutually vertex-disjoint walks

      This section presents results of [HGB18] that are key to this thesis. In
      this work, the authors revisited the notion of generic rank — originally
      introduced in [Dav77] — and applied it to the problem of generic global
      identifiability, in the case where all transfer functions are unknown, and all
      nodes ares excited (respectively, measured).
         The full-rankness of a parameterized matrix is a generic property. More
      precisely, the fact that a matrix attains its maximal rank is a generic property.

                                                                                | 30
          Characterization with mutually vertex-disjoint walks | 3.1




     Fig. 3.1 Illustration of the notion of generic rank, Example 3.2.


This maximal rank is referred to as the generic rank of the matrix [Dav77].

Definition 3.1. For set M and naturals n1 , n2 , let M : M → Cn1 ×n2 be an
analytical matrix function. The generic rank (also denoted structural rank
in the literature) of M, denoted grank ( M), is the maximum rank that M( x )
attains for any value of x ∈ M:

                         grank ( M ) = max rank ( M ( x )).
                                         x ∈M

   As will be proved in Lemma 5.3, the generic rank of M is also the rank
obtained for almost all x, i.e. for all x except possibly those lying on a set of
dimension lower than dim M.
                                                
                                     x1      0
Example 3.2. The matrix M =                        has generic rank 2. Its rank
                                     0 x1 − x2
drops on { x1 = 0} ∪ { x1 = x2 }, see Fig. 3.1.

    The notion of generic rank plays a central role in our work and admits
a meaningful graphical interpretation within our network model. In par-
ticular, it connects closely to the notion of mutually vertex-disjoint walks,
introduced in Section 2.2.1. The proposition below is from [dW91, HGB18],
and is illustrated in Example 3.4.

Proposition 3.3. Let I , J be two sets of nodes of the associated graph of a network
matrix G (z, θ ). Let TI ,J (z, θ ) be the restriction of T (z, θ ) = ( I − G (z, θ ))−1
to the rows corresponding to J and the columns corresponding to I . Then, the
generic rank of TI ,J (z, θ ) over parameter θ is the maximum number of mutually
vertex-disjoint walks from J to I , denoted β J →I .


                                                                                 | 31
          Characterization with mutually vertex-disjoint walks | 3.1




                 J                                            I




Fig. 3.2 One can route up to three vertex-disjoint walks from J to I (in
pink), i.e. β J →I = 3.


Example 3.4. Consider the network depicted in Fig. 3.2. One can compute
the generic rank of the matrix T corresponding to this network: it is 3. And
we verify in Fig. 3.2 that one can route up to three vertex-disjoint walks
from J to I (in pink), i.e. β J →I = 3.
Remark 3.5. In [HGB18] and related works, the notion of mutually vertex-
disjoint paths is commonly used. In contrast, throughout this thesis we rely
on the notion of walks rather than paths. Recall from Section 2.2.1 that a
path is a walk in which all nodes are distinct — in contrast to a walk, where
repetition of nodes (and edges) is allowed. This distinction is important
in our context, since in Chapter 8 it is specifically the notion of walks
that naturally arises in the characterization of generic local identifiability.
Nevertheless, the difference is often negligible in practice, as a path can
always be extracted from a walk.

    The work in [HGB18] provides a necessary and sufficient condition
for the generic global identifiability of all outgoing transfer functions of a
node, when all transfer functions are unknown, and all nodes are excited.
This condition is derived using the notion of generic rank, with the aid
of Proposition 3.3, and is formulated in terms of mutually vertex-disjoint
walks in the associated graph. This graph-theoretic formulation underscores
the inherently topological nature of generic identifiability.

Theorem 3.6. The transfer functions from node i to its out-neighbors Wi+ can
generically all be globally identified when exciting all nodes and measuring nodes
C if and only if β W + →C = Wi+ .
                   i

   A more general version of Theorem 3.6, which addresses the generic
global identifiability of any subset of Wi+ , is presented in [HGB18]. The
dual result, applicable under full excitation, is stated below.

                                                                           | 32
                                      Algorithm for excitation allocation | 3.2

      Theorem 3.7. The transfer functions to node j from its in-neighbors W j− can
      generically all be globally identified when exciting nodes B and measuring all nodes
      if and only if β B→W − = W j− .
                           j




3.2     Algorithm for excitation allocation

      This section presents results from [CSVdH21, DSC+ 22], which form the
      foundation of the algorithm developed in Sections 11.2.3 and 11.2.4. These
      works reformulate the vertex-disjoint walk condition of Theorem 3.7 in
      terms of coverings of subgraphs called SIMUGs. This reformulation enables
      the design of an excitation allocation algorithm that guarantees global
      identifiability under full measurement. Definitions 3.8 and 3.9 introduce
      the notion of SIMUG.
      Notation: Consider a graph G . We denote by V (G) its set of nodes and by
      E(G) its set of edges.
      Definition 3.8. A connected directed graph T , with |V (T )| ≥ 2, is called
      a (directed) multi-rooted graph if there exists a path from each node in a
      nonempty set of roots Υ(T ) to every node i ∈ V (T ).
         We focus on a specific class of multi-rooted graphs in which each node
      has at most one incoming parametrized edge.
      Definition 3.9. A multi-rooted graph T is called a single-source identifiable
      multi-rooted graph (SIMUG) if all nodes j ∈ V (T ) satisfy |W j◦− | ≤ 1, where
      W j◦− ≜ {i | (i, j) ∈ E ◦ } is the set of in-neighbours of j through unknown
      transfer functions.
          Edge-disjoint SIMUGs are defined below. This notion will be seen to be
      closely related to the concept of vertex-disjoint walks.
      Definition 3.10. Consider two multi-rooted graphs T1 and T2 as subgraphs
      of a directed graph G . T1 and T2 are called edge-disjoint in G if the following
      two conditions hold:
         1. T1 and T2 have no common edges;
         2. For each node j in T1 ∪ T2 , all outgoing edges of j in T1 ∪ T2 belong to
            either T1 or to T2 . Hence T1 and T2 do not share the same root or any
            common internal node.

                                                                                   | 33
                                 Algorithm for excitation allocation | 3.2

    [CSVdH21] highlights how the notion of disjoint SIMUGs is closely
related to that of vertex-disjoint walks. Consider T1 and T2 as two disjoint
SIMUGs in G . For any j ∈ V (T1 ) ∩ V (T2 ), if |W j− | ≥ 2, then there exist
two in-neighbors of j located in T1 and T2 separately. Due to the fact that
distinct SIMUGs cannot share any common root or internal vertex, we can
find two vertex-disjoint walks in the union T1 ∪ T2 , starting from two roots
in T1 and T2 respectively, and leading to two distinct in-neighbors of j.
    Next, the concept of disjoint-edge covering for a directed graph is intro-
duced.

Definition 3.11. Consider a directed graph G , and let Π ≜ {T1 , T2 , . . . , Tn }
be a collection of edge-disjoint SIMUGs of G . The edges in a set Ẽ ⊆ E(G)
are covered by Π, if E(T1 ) ∪ E(T2 ) ∪ · · · ∪ E(Tn ) = Ẽ , and Π is called an
edge-disjoint SIMUG covering of Ẽ .

   By combining Theorem 3.7 for all nodes W with the notions introduced
above, we obtain Theorem 3.12 — a sufficient condition for generic global
identifiability based on disjoint SIMUGs. This result forms the theoretical
foundation for the synthesis method presented in Algorithm 1.

Theorem 3.12. Consider a network G (z, θ ) = G • (z, θ • ) + G ◦ (z, θ ◦ ) with ex-
citations B , measurements on all nodes and let Gnet = (W , E ) be its associated
graph. The network is generically globally identifiable if there exists an edge-disjoint
SIMUG covering of E , such that at least one of the roots of each SIMUG is excited.

    Algorithm 1 provides a procedure to compute a set of excitations that
guarantees generic global identifiability when all nodes are measured. It
first constructs an edge-disjoint SIMUG covering, then assigns one exci-
tation per SIMUG, at one of its roots, and finally prunes unnecessary ex-
citations by testing whether global identifiability is preserved upon their
removal. Note that there is no guarantee on the minimality of the number
of excitations obtained by Algorithm 1. This algorithm serves as a basis for
the method developed in Section 11.2.4.
Notations: W j◦− is the set of in-neighbours of j through unknown transfer
functions. β I→J is the maximum number of mutually vertex-disjoint walks
from I to J .




                                                                                | 34
                                   Algorithm for excitation allocation | 3.2

      Algorithm 1 Allocation of Excitations in Full Measurement
      Require: The associated graph Gnet = (W , {E • , E ◦ }).
      Result: A set of excitations B such that the network is generically globally
         identifiable under full measurement.
       1: Construct an edge-disjoint SIMUG covering Π with Algorithm 1
          of [CSVdH21], adapted to the case with known transfer functions
          [DSC+ 22].
       2: Build excitation set B by selecting one root from each SIMUG in Π,
          except the SIMUGs that are composed of known edges only.
       3: For each bi ∈ B : remove bi from B if β B\{b }→W ◦−
                                                         i
                                                                     = W j◦− for all
                                                                 j
          j ∈ W.
       4: return B .



3.2.1 Without known modules: pseudotrees
      The notions and results presented in Section 3.2 are derived in the presence
      of both known and unknown modules, and originate from [DSC+ 22]. A
      preliminary version of this work, focusing on networks with only unknown
      modules, is presented in [CSVdH21]. The approaches in both works are
      closely related: the SIMUGs in [DSC+ 22] are replaced by pseudotrees in
      [CSVdH21], and the algorithms for merging them differ slightly due to the
      absence of known modules in the latter. Nonetheless, [CSVdH21] proposes
      an algorithm — conceptually very similar to Algorithm 1 — that constructs
      an excitation set ensuring identifiability in the absence of known modules,
      based on an edge-disjoint pseudotree covering. This algorithm forms the
      foundation for the method developed in Section 11.2.3.
         Similarly to [HGB18], all results of this section can be derived analo-
      gously for the dual setting of full excitation, see [CSVdH21] for details.




                                                                              | 35
           PART I
Algebraic characterization of
     local identifiability
      Local identifiability and local
                                                                            4
                           injectivity


      W
                 E first introduce the notion of local identifiability, a weaker notion
                 than global identifiability, but which is amenable to linear analy-
                 sis. Local identifiability is then formulated as the local injectivity
      of a specific function. Finally, we establish that, generically, working with
      scalar modules on the edges is equivalent to working with analytic transfer
      functions. This chapter is based on [LH24] and its preliminary version
      [LH20].



4.1     Local identifiability

      The graph-theoretical conditions for generic global identifiability obtained
      under full excitation or measurement (i.e. when B or C = I) in Chapter 3
      from [HGB18, CSVdH21, DSC+ 22] were made possible by a linear algebraic
      reformulation of the generic global identifiability questions. No equivalent
      reformulation is available for partial excitations and measurements. Hence
      we will work on a weaker notion amenable to linear analysis: local iden-
      tifiability, which corresponds to identifiability provided that G (z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • ))
      is sufficiently close to G (z; (θ ◦ , θ • )). Definition 4.1 extends to networked
      systems the notion of local identifiability introduced in [Lju99].


                                                                                   | 37
                                                     Identifiability and injectivity | 4.2

      Definition 4.1. Consider a network G (z; θ ) = G ◦ (z; θ ◦ ) + G • (z; θ • ) with ex-
      citations and measurements matrices ( B, C ). The unknown transfer function
      Gij◦ is locally identifiable at θ = (θ ◦ , θ • ) if there exists a continuous positive
      function ϵ : C → R0+ such that for all θ̃ ◦ ∈ Θ◦ , there holds:
                                   −1                               −1
       If C I − G z; (θ ◦ , θ • )        B = C I − G z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • )        B for all z, then

                either Gij◦ (z; (θ ◦ , θ • )) = Gij◦ (z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • )) for all z
                     or G (z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • )) ̸∈ Ball( G (z; (θ ◦ , θ • )), ϵ(z)) for all z.
                                                                                               (4.1)

      Gij◦ is generically locally identifiable if it is locally identifiable at almost all
      θ ∈ Θ. This definition of identifiability of a single transfer function extends
      to a set of transfer functions by replacing Gij◦ in the second line of (4.1).

          Local identifiability can be interpreted as no ambiguity with the
      G (z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • )) lying in a local neighborhood of G (z; (θ ◦ , θ • )), while global
      identifiability is no ambiguity with all G (z; (θ̃ ◦ , θ • )). A similar concept in
      control theory is local stability, which refers to the stability of a system in a
      neighborhood around an equilibrium point.
          Local identifiability is a necessary condition for global identifiability,
      as it represents the same notion restricted to a local neighborhood. It is
      a priori a weaker notion, yet no example of network generically locally
      identifiable but not generically globally identifiable is known to the authors,
      despite having checked all networks with up to five unknown transfer
      functions and no known transfer functions, see Section 12.2. In the case
      of full excitation or full measurement, the two notions are equivalent, see
      Proposition 5.13 in Section 5.2.



4.2     Identifiability and injectivity

      The considered local identifiability questions can be reformulated in terms
      of local injectivity. In this Section 4.2, we consider the scalar case: we work
      with complex values Aij ∈ C instead of transfer functions Gij (z; θ ). To
      highlight the distinction with transfer functions, we refer to those scalar
      complex values as modules. The scalar case will be proved to be equiv-
      alent to the standard “transfer function case” in Section 4.3. Matrices

a• = nonzeros( A• ). Conversely, we denote by Ā( a◦ ; a• ) = A the network

Definition 4.3. For sets M, N , and ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , dim M}, the function
f : M → N is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at x ∈ M
if there exists ϵ > 0 such that for all x̃ ∈ Ball( x, ϵ), there holds

4.3     Reduction to the scalar case

      In this Section, we prove that when considered generically, the notions of
      Definition 4.3 and Definition 4.4 are equivalent for an analytic function. We
      first present three lemmas that support the proof of Theorem 4.11, which
      establishes the equivalence aforementioned.
         Lemma 4.8 characterizes the intersection between a closed complex
      analytic variety and a complex analytic trajectory. It is illustrated in Fig. 4.1.
      Lemma 4.8. Let M be a manifold, S ⊂ M be a closed complex analytic variety
      of lower dimension than M, and g : C → M a complex analytic function. Either
      Im g is entirely included in S , or the intersection between Im g and S is a discrete
      set of points.

      Proof. A complex analytic variety is the zero locus of a set of complex
      analytic functions. Denote those functions s1 , . . . , ss for S , with si : M → C
      for each i. We have S = s1−1 (0) ∩ s2−1 (0) ∩ . . . ∩ s−        1             −1
                                                                    s (0), where si (0)
      denotes the zero locus of function si . Since composition preserves the
      analytic character of functions, si ◦ g is also analytic. Hence:

                                                                                         | 41
                                       Reduction to the scalar case | 4.3



                Im g’                               Im g




                     Fig. 4.1 Illustration of Lemma 4.8.


   • either for all i, si ◦ g is the zero function, hence Im g ⊂ S

   • or there is i such that si ◦ g is zero only on a discrete set of points
     z j ∈ C. At those points z j , g(z j ) lies in the zero locus of si . Thus, Im g
     intersects si−1 (0) at a discrete set of points { g(z j )}, and Im g ∩ S is a
     discrete set of points.



   Lemma 4.9 ensures that for each point u in a ϵ/2-neighborhood of
y ∈ M, scalar-coordinate-injectivity holds in a ϵ/2-neighborhood of u.
This lemma, illustrated in Fig. 4.2, is exploited by the proof of Lemma 4.10.
Notations y, w are just elements of manifold M in Lemma 4.9, they should
not be confused with the node signals of our network model (2.1).

Lemma 4.9. Let f : M → N be a C ∞ function where M and N are smooth
manifolds, M or N has finite dimension, M is open and for all y ∈ M : ∇ f (y)
has the same rank and ker ∇ f (y) ⊥ eℓ (where eℓ denotes the standard basis vector
filled with zeros except 1 at the ℓ-th entry). We have :

      For all y ∈ M, there exists ϵ > 0 such that :

           For each u ∈ Ball(y, ϵ/2), we have :
                For each w ∈ Ball(u, ϵ/2), there holds :

                                    f ( w ) = f ( u ) ⇒ wℓ = uℓ .               (4.8)


Proof. This proof relies on the rank theorem [AMR12], which states that if
∇ f has constant rank k in a manifold of dimension m, then there are local
coordinates where m − k variables are redundant and can be eliminated.

                                                                              | 42
                                      Reduction to the scalar case | 4.3




                    Fig. 4.2 Illustration of Lemma 4.9.


   Consider y ∈ M, and take ϵ > 0 such that:

   • Ball(y, ϵ) ⊆ M (which is possible for any y since M is open).

   • The local coordinates provided by the rank theorem [AMR12] applied
     on y are valid on the whole Ball(y, ϵ), i.e. each point χ ∈ Ball(y, ϵ) can
     be expressed in the local coordinates provided by the rank theorem
     applied on y.

    Consider a point u ∈ Ball(y, ϵ/2). The smoothness of f and constant
rank of ∇ f allow the application of the subimmersion theorem [AMR12]. In
short, this theorem states that the level set of an image n0 by such function is
a submanifold of M, and its tangent space coincides with the kernel of the
gradient. Here, we take n0 = f (u) to make the connection with injectivity,
and the theorem yields that for all u, f −1 ( f (u)) is a submanifold of M with

               Tw f −1 ( f (u)) = ker ∇ f (w)   ∀ w ∈ f −1 ( f (u)),       (4.9)

where Tw A denotes the tangent space to manifold A at w.
Combining ker ∇ f (u) ⊥ eℓ with (5.2) yields Tw f −1 ( f (u)) ⊥ eℓ for all
w ∈ f −1 ( f (u)).
   Take a point w ∈ Ball(u, ϵ/2) ∩ f −1 ( f (u)), and assume that there is a
path lying in f −1 ( f (u)) between u and w. At every point of this path, the
tangent space is orthogonal to direction ℓ, so the derivative along the path


                                                                         | 43
                                              Reduction to the scalar case | 4.3

is zero in the ℓ-coordinate. Integrating along the path gives a zero increase
in ℓ-coordinate, hence uℓ = wℓ .
    To complete the proof, we need to show that there is a path lying in
f −1 ( f (u)) between u and w. Such path can be found with the rank theo-
rem [AMR12], which states that for all p in the manifold, there exist local
coordinates centered at p and f ( p) in which f (q) = (q1 , . . . , qk , 0, . . . , 0), i.e.
the last m − k entries are zero, for any q in a local neighbourhood of p. In
particular, two points have the same image by f if and only if the k first
entries of their image by f are equal in those local coordinates.
    For the rest of this proof, we work in the local coordinates provided by
this theorem, centered at p = y. Recall that ϵ is such that each point u ∈
Ball(y, ϵ) can be expressed in those local coordinates. Since u ∈ Ball(y, ϵ/2)
and w ∈ Ball(u, ϵ/2), the points u, w ∈ Ball(y, ϵ), and both u, w can be
expressed in those local coordinates. The rank theorem gives f (u) =
(u1 , . . . , uk , 0, . . . , 0) and f (w) = (w1 , . . . , wk , 0, . . . , 0) in the local coordi-
nates. Since w lies in the manifold f −1 ( f (u)), it must have the same image
as u: f (w) = f (u), and w1 = u1 , . . . , wk = uk . An example of path between
u and w is given by (u1 , . . . , uk , (1 − λ)uk+1 + λwx+1 , . . . , (1 − λ)u N + λw N ),
with λ ∈ [0, 1]. Any point of this path lies in Ball(y, ϵ), and applying the
function f on any point of this path gives f (u) in the local coordinates by
the rank theorem, showing that the path is indeed included in the manifold
f −1 ( f (u)).

    Lemma 4.10 ensures the existence of a continuous positive function ϵ
inside which we have scalar-coordinate-injectivity. This is one of the keys
for proving Theorem 4.11.

Lemma 4.10. Let M be a smooth, open and finite-dimensional manifold, N be
a smooth manifold, and S ⊂ M be a closed complex analytic variety of lower
dimension than M. Let f : M → N be a C ∞ function, such that for all x ∈
M \ S : ∇ f ( x ) has the same rank and f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for
coordinate ℓ. Let g : C → M be a complex analytic function such that Im g ̸⊂ S
(but Im g ∩ S may be nonempty), and let gℓ bet its ℓ-th entry.
    There exists a continuous positive function ϵ : C → R0+ , such that for each
z ∈ C, we have scalar-coordinate-injectivity for coordinate ℓ at g(z) on the whole
Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)), i.e. for all x̃ ∈ Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)), there holds

                             f ( x̃ ) = f ( g(z)) ⇒ x̃ℓ = gℓ (z).                         (4.10)


                                                                                          | 44
                                     Reduction to the scalar case | 4.3




            Fig. 4.3 Illustration of the sets Ωn of Lemma 4.10.


Proof. We denote MS ≜ M \ S . The strategy of the proof is to apply
Lemma 4.9 on MS , then define the sets Ωn , illustrated in Fig. 4.3, extract a
finite subcover of those sets and construct a continuous function ϵ from this
subcover, as illustrated in Fig. 4.4.
    The set M is open and S is closed, hence MS is open. Since we assume
that for all y ∈ MS : ∇ f has the same rank and f is locally scalar-coordinate-
injective for coordinate ℓ, we have ker ∇ f (y) ⊥ eℓ for all y ∈ MS by
Lemma 5.1. Therefore, Lemma 4.9 can be applied on MS : for all y ∈ MS ,
there exists ρy > 0 such that:

      For each u ∈ Ball( x, ρy /2), we have :

           For each w ∈ Ball(u, ρy /2), there holds :

                                f ( w ) = f ( u ) ⇒ wℓ = uℓ .            (4.11)

    We define the sets Ωn , which are illustrated in Fig. 4.3: Ωn ≜ {y ∈
M s.t. ∥y − χ∥ ≥ 1/n ∀ χ ∈ S and ∥y∥ ≤ n} for n ∈ N. For each n ∈ N ,
Ωn is closed, bounded and finite-dimensional, hence compact. Define the
sets Ψn ≜ {Ball(y, ρy /2) | y ∈ Ωn }. Ψn is an infinite cover of Ωn , i.e.
Ωn ⊆ Υ∈Ψn Υ. Since Ωn is compact, we can extract a finite subcover Φn
       S

from Ψn : we take Φn ⊂ Ψn such that Φn is finite and Ωn ⊆ Υ∈Φn Υ.
                                                            S

    Since Φn is finite, there is a minimum radius of all the balls that compose
Φn , which we denote ϕn . Take a point χ ∈ int Ωn . Since Φn is a cover of Ωn ,

there is Ball(v, ρv /2) ∈ Φn such that χ ∈ Ball(v, ρv /2), i.e. ∥χ − v∥ < ρv /2.
Also, by definition, ϕn ≤ ρv /2. Now take Ball(χ, ϕn ). This ball is entirely

can be bounded as: ρv − ∥χ − v∥ ≥ ρv − 2v = 2v ≥ ϕn , which is equivalent

x̃ ∈ Ball(χ, r ), there holds f ( x̃ ) = f (χ) ⇒ x̃ℓ = χℓ .
    Define ωn = min(ϕ1 , ϕ2 , . . . , ϕn ). Define the function γ, illustrated in

we can easily construct a continuous positive lower bound by linking linear

gives the function ϵ̃, a lower bound of γ which is continuous, positive and

    We define the function η : M → R0+ : y → max (∥y∥ , 1/distS (y)) ,
where distS (y) is the distance between y and S , defined as distS : M →
R+ : y → inf {∥y − χ∥ : χ ∈ S}. The function ϵ̃(d) is continuous by
construction. We know that the function distance to a set is continuous,
hence so is the function distS , and so is its inverse 1/distS . The function
g : C → M is continuous since it is analytic, thus its norm ∥ g∥ is also
continuous. Therefore, the function η is continuous, and so is the function
ϵ : C → R0+ : z → ϵ̃ ( η ( g(z) ) ).
    This function ϵ is built such that for each z ∈ C, ϵ(z) ≤ ϕn for the
smallest n such that g(z) ∈ Ωn . Hence, we have scalar-coordinate-injectivity
for coordinate ℓ at g(z) on the whole Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)), i.e. for all points
x̃ ∈ Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)), there holds f ( x̃ ) = f ( g(z)) ⇒ x̃ℓ = gℓ (z).


   Theorem 4.11 is one of the main results of this thesis. Building on
Lemmas 4.8, 4.9 and 4.10, it establishes that, when considered generically,
Definitions 4.3 and 4.4 are equivalent for analytic functions. Theorem
4.11 if stated here in its most general form, for any analytic function f . It
will subsequently be formulated for parameterized analytic functions in
Corollary 4.13.

Theorem 4.11. Let M be a smooth, open and finite-dimensional manifold, N be a
smooth manifold, f : M → N be an analytic function and ℓ ∈ {1, . . . , dim M}.
Let S ⊂ M be a closed complex analytic variety of lower dimension than M, such
that ∇ f ( x ) reaches its maximal rank (hence constant) for all x ∈ M \ S . The
function f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at all x ∈ M \ S
if and only if f is locally functional-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at all
analytic function g : C → M such that Im g ̸⊂ S (but Im g ∩ S may be
nonempty).

Proof. First, sufficiency is trivial. Assume that f is locally functional-
coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at all function g(z) except those with
Im g ⊂ S , where S is on a lower-dimensional complex analytic variety.
Consider a point x ̸∈ S . Take the constant function ḡ(z) = x ∀ z. There
is a function ϵ(z) such that f is locally functional-coordinate-injective for
coordinate ℓ at function ḡ. Take ϵ̌ = minz ϵ(z). With this ϵ̌, f is also locally
scalar-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at x.
   For necessity, the strategy of the proof is to consider an analytic function
g(z) that does not entirely lie in S . Lemma 4.10 ensures the existence of


                                                                            | 47
                                        Reduction to the scalar case | 4.3

a continuous positive function ϵ ensuring scalar-coordinate-injectivity on
the whole Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)). Then we consider another analytic function g̃
such that for all z, we have f ( g̃(z)) = f ( g(z)). We prove that for almost
all z0 , either (i) g̃(z0 ) is close enough to g(z0 ) and local scalar-coordinate-
injectivity applies, or (ii) it is too far and local scalar-coordinate-injectivity
does not apply. Exploiting the fact that ϵ is continuous and g and g̃ are
continuous analytic functions yields g̃ℓ (z) = gℓ (z) for all z.
     Assume that f is generically locally scalar-coordinate-injective for coor-
dinate ℓ. Take an analytic function g(z) that does not entirely lie in S , but
may intersect it, i.e. Im g ̸⊂ S , but Im g ∩ S can be nonempty. Lemma 4.10
ensures the existence of a continuous positive function ϵ : C → R0+ , such
that for each z ∈ C, Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)) ∩ S = ∅, and such that f is scalar-
coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at g(z) on the whole Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)),
i.e. for all x̃ ∈ Ball( g(z), ϵ(z)), there holds

                         f ( x̃ ) = f ( g(z)) ⇒ x̃ℓ = gℓ (z).                (4.12)

      Consider an analytic function g̃ : C → M such that for all z, we have
f ( g̃(z)) = f ( g(z)). Consider z0 ∈ C such that g(z0 ) ̸∈ S . Two scenarios are
possible:

  (i) either g̃(z0 ) ∈ Ball( g(z0 ), ϵ(z0 )) and local scalar-coordinate-injectivity
      applies: we have x̃ℓ (z0 ) = xℓ (z0 )
  (ii) or g̃(z0 ) is too far from g(z0 ) and local scalar-coordinate-injectivity
       does not apply: we have g̃(z0 ) ̸∈ Ball( g(z0 ), ϵ(z0 )).

     Since f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at all x
except possibly on S , this holds for all g(z0 ) except possibly on S , and since
we assumed g(z) not entirely included in S , applying Lemma 4.8 yields
that it holds for all z0 except possibly on a complex analytic variety of lower
dimension than S .
     At this point of the proof we have, for almost all z, either (i) or (ii). In
the last paragraph, we prove that we either have (i) for all z, or (ii) for all z.
     Recall that ϵ, g and g̃ are continuous functions. Hence if there is z0 such
that g(z0 ) ̸∈ S and g̃(z0 ) ∈ Ball( g(z0 ), ϵ(z0 )), then by continuity of ϵ, g and
g̃, there is Z > 0 such that for all ζ ∈ Ball(z0 , Z ), we have g(ζ ) ̸∈ S and
g̃(ζ ) ∈ Ball( g(ζ ), ϵ(ζ )).

   Thus, we also have g̃ℓ (ζ ) = gℓ (ζ ) by local scalar-coordinate-injectivity.


                                                                             | 48
                                           Identifiability in the scalar case | 4.4

          Since g̃ℓ (z) and gℓ (z) are analytic functions, their difference g̃ℓ (z) − gℓ (z)
      is also analytic and is either the zero function, or it is zero only on a complex
      analytic variety of lower dimension. Given the fact that g̃ℓ (ζ ) = gℓ (ζ ) on
      Ball(z0 , Z ), it must be the zero function. Thus g̃ℓ (z) = gℓ (z) for all z.

      Note 4.12. Although we work with a rationally parameterized model (2.2)
      — a choice motivated by real-world applications and standard practice in
      System Identification [Lju99] — our results extend to arbitrary analytic
      functions. Indeed, Theorem 4.11 applies to any analytic functions, not just
      rational ones.
          Applying Theorem 4.11 to parameterized analytic functions yields Corol-
      lary 4.13, which establishes the equivalence between the notions of Defini-
      tions 4.5 and 4.6, when considered generically. In Section 4.4, Corollary 4.13
                                                                       ◦     •
      will be applied on h defined in (4.2), with manifold M = Cm × Cm \ Dh ,
      and manifold N = CnB ·nC . Manifolds M and N could be defined more
      specifically in Corollary 4.13, but we keep the statement in its most general
      form.
      Corollary 4.13. Let M◦ , M• be smooth, open and finite-dimensional manifolds,
      D f be a closed set, N be a smooth manifold, Φ◦ , Φ• be sets and denote M ≜ M◦ ×
      M• \ D f . Let f : M → N be an analytic function and ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , dim M◦ }.
      Let S ⊂ M be a closed complex analytic variety of lower dimension than M,
      such that ∇ f ( x ) reaches its maximal rank (hence constant) for all x ∈ M \ S .
      The function f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ at all x ∈
      M \ S if and only if f is locally functional-coordinate-injective for coordinate ℓ of
      paramaterized analytic function g : C × Φ◦ × Φ• → M◦ × M• at all (ϕ◦ , ϕ• ) ∈
      Φ◦ × Φ• such that Im g(· ; (ϕ◦ , ϕ• )) ̸⊂ S (but Im g(· ; (ϕ◦ , ϕ• )) ∩ S may be
      nonempty).



4.4     Identifiability in the scalar case

      Corollary 4.13 establishes that, when considered generically, the results
      obtained for scalar-coordinate injectivity also apply to functional-coordinate
      injectivity for analytic parameterized functions. Consequently, in order to
      ease the analysis, we will work in the scalar setting in the remainder of this
      thesis:
         • The transfer functions Gij (z; θ ) are now modeled by scalar values
           Aij ∈ C, and will be denoted by modules.

                                                                                     | 49
                                         Identifiability in the scalar case | 4.4

   • Matrices A, A◦ , A• ∈ C L× L replace matrices of transfer functions
     G (z; θ ), G ◦ (z; θ ◦ ), G • (z; θ • ) respectively, with same nonzero entries
     respectively.

   • Vectors of unknown and known modules a◦ = nonzeros( A◦ ), a• =
                                                              ◦         •
     nonzeros( A• ) are vectors of scalar values, i.e. a◦ ∈ Cm , a• ∈ Cm .
                                ◦  •
     Vector a = ( a◦ , a• ) ∈ Cm +m collects all unknown and known mod-
     ules.

   • The function Ā( a◦ ; a• ) rearranges vector a = ( a◦ , a• ) in the network
     topology, i.e. Ā( a◦ ; a• ) = A = A◦ + A• .

   • Unless otherwise specified, mentions of local (coordinate)-injectivity
     should be understood as referring to the scalar version.

   In the scalar case, the notions of global and local identifiability intro-
duced in Definitions 2.2 and 4.1 take the following form.

Definition 4.14. Consider a network A = A◦ + A• with excitations and
measurements matrices ( B, C ). The unknown module Aij◦ is globally identifi-

   The notion of local scalar-coordinate-injectivity that was introduced in
Definition 4.5 is a generalization of the concept of local identifiability in the
scalar case, presented in Definition 4.15. This is formalized in Lemma 4.16.

Lemma 4.16. Consider a network A = A◦ + A• with excitations and measure-
ments matrices ( B, C ). The unknown module Aij◦ is locally identifiable at ( a◦ , a• ) if
and only if the corresponding function h defined in (4.2) is locally scalar-coordinate-
injective for coordinate ℓ at ( a◦ , a• ), where ℓ is the index corresponding to Aij◦ in
vector a◦ .

Proof. Substituting the expression of function h introduced in (4.2) in
Definition 4.5 of local scalar-coordinate-injectivity yields precisely Defi-
nition 4.15.

   Combining the results of Lemma 4.7, Corollary 4.13, and Lemma 4.16
gives Theorem 4.17, which establishes the equivalence between generic
local identifiability in the transfer function setting and in the scalar case.

Theorem 4.17. Consider a dynamic network G (z; θ ) = G ◦ (z; θ ◦ ) + G • (z; θ • )
and its corresponding scalar network A = A◦ + A• , with same excitations and
measurements. The unknown transfer function Gij◦ is generically locally identifiable
(in the sense of Definition 4.1) if and only if the corresponding module Aij◦ is
generically locally identifiable (in the sense of Definition 4.15).

Proof. First, Lemma 4.7 establishes that the unknown transfer function Gij◦ is
locally identifiable at θ = (θ ◦ , θ • ) if and only if the corresponding function
h defined in (4.2) is locally functional-coordinate-injective for coordinate
ℓ of function gnet defined in (4.7) at θ = (θ ◦ , θ • ), where ℓ is the index
corresponding to Gij◦ in the image of gnet . Second, Corollary 4.13 demon-
strates that when considered generically, functional-coordinate-injectivity
for parameterized functions of the form of gnet is equivalent to scalar-
coordinate-injectivity. Finally, Lemma 4.16 shows that the notion of lo-
cal scalar-coordinate-injectivity applied to function h gives precisely local
identifiability in the scalar setting.

    Theorem 4.17 provides a significant simplification: rather than analyzing
transfer functions directly, we can equivalently work with complex-valued
modules on the edges, which can be interpreted as edge weights. This
reformulation reveals the inherently topological nature of generic local
identifiability, thereby justifying our focus on this concept and motivating
the methodology adopted. The following chapters will further reinforce

                                                                                  | 51
                                Identifiability in the scalar case | 4.4

this initial intuition regarding the topological character of generic local
identifiability.




                                                                     | 52
     Algebraic characterizations
                                                                    5
I
    N this chapter and throughout the remainder of the thesis, we restrict our
    attention to the scalar case, as Theorem 4.17 establishes the equivalence
    between generic local identifiability in the transfer function setting and
in the scalar setting. We begin by deriving an algebraic characterization
of local scalar-coordinate injectivity using tools from manifold theory. As
part of this analysis, we also show that scalar-coordinate injectivity is a
pseudo-generic property. By applying this characterization to the function
h (defined in (4.2)), we obtain an algebraic characterization of local identifia-
bility and demonstrate that it too is a pseudo-generic property. This leads to
necessary and sufficient conditions for generic local identifiability, both (i)
for a single unknown module and (ii) for all unknown modules in the net-
work. Leveraging the pseudo-generic nature of local identifiability, together
with the transition from the transfer function setting to the scalar setting,
these algebraic conditions can be verified numerically with probability 1
by evaluating them on a randomly sampled instance of the network. We
develop an algorithm based on this principle, which efficiently computes
the generic local identifiability of any given network. This chapter is based
on [LH24] and its preliminary version [LH20].




                                                                          | 53
                          Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1


5.1     Algebraic characterization of local injectivity

      In this section, we establish the equivalence of local scalar-coordinate-
      injectivity with an orthogonality relation of the gradient: see equation (5.1).
      We further demonstrate that local scalar-coordinate-injectivity is pseudo-
      generic by proving that (5.1) holds pseudo-generically. To this end, we
      present Lemmas 5.1, 5.3, and 5.4, which collectively support the proof of
      Proposition 5.7. In Section 5.2, we will show how local scalar-coordinate-
      injectivity connects to local identifiability, thereby establishing the pseudo-
      generic nature of the latter as well.


                Lemma 5.1




                Lemma 5.3                 Corollary 5.5            Proposition 5.7




                Lemma 5.4                 Corollary 5.6



          Intuitively, if the problem was one-dimensional, the derivative could
      be used to assess local injectivity. Indeed, if the function is constant —
      hence has a zero derivative — it cannot be locally injective. Conversely, h
      would be locally injective at ( a◦ , a• ) if h′ ( a◦ ; a• ) ̸= 0, where the derivative
      is taken with respect to the variable a◦ and the parameters a• are fixed.
      In the multidimensional case, the condition on the derivative becomes:
                                                         ◦
      ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) · δ ̸= 0 for any nonzero δ ∈ Cm , i.e. if ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) has a trivial
      kernel. Similarly, h would be coordinate-injective at ( a◦ , a• ) for module
                                                   ◦
      ℓ if ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) · δ ̸= 0 for any δ ∈ Cm with δℓ ̸= 0, i.e. if the kernel of
      ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) is orthogonal to eℓ , the standard basis vector filled with zeros
      except 1 at the ℓ-th entry.
         This kernel condition is sufficient for local (coordinate-)injectivity, but
      another assumption is required to achieve necessity. For example, the
      real function g( x ) = x3 is injective, but g′ (0) = 0 admits a non-trivial
      kernel, which contradicts the necessity of our previous statement. The

                                                                                     | 54
                   Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1

problem is that the derivative of g vanishes at 0, while being nonzero
everywhere else. Adding an assumption on the conservation of information
contained in the derivative (i.e. the rank of its gradient) resolves that issue.
Lemma 5.1 shows that the intuitive idea behind the gradient is valid when
∇ f has constant rank. We will then see in Proposition 5.7 how this rather
constraining assumption can be removed provided one is only interested in
local injectivity at almost all x, i.e. in a generic manner.
Lemma 5.1. Let f : M → N be a C ∞ function where M and N are smooth
manifolds, M or N has finite dimension, ∇ f ( x ) has the same rank for every
x ∈ M, and ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , dim M}. There holds
  (a) For all x ∈ M: if f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for ℓ at x, then

                                        ker ∇ f ( x ) ⊥ eℓ ,                      (5.1)

      where eℓ denotes the standard basis vector filled with zeros except 1 at the
      ℓ-th entry.
  (b) If (5.1) holds for all x ∈ M, then f is locally scalar-coordinate-injective for
      ℓ at all x ∈ M.
Note 5.2. Observe the difference between (a) and (b) in Lemma 5.1. (a) can
be summarized as: ∀ x : (4.3) ⇒ (5.1), while (b) would be summarized as:
(5.1) ∀ x ⇒ (4.3) ∀ x. The formulation of (a) is stronger: the implication holds
at each point of the manifold. On the other hand, for (b), (5.1) must hold at
all points of the manifold.
    To illustrate this difference, consider the following example. Suppose
that (4.3) holds for all points in M except for one exceptional point x0 .
In that case, (a) still guarantees that (5.1) is satisfied for every point in
M \ { x0 }. On the other hand, for (b), the existence of a single point x1
where (5.1) fails is sufficient to invalidate the assumption, so the statement
does not apply and provides no information.

Proof. The smoothness of f and constant rank of ∇ f allow the application
of the subimmersion theorem [AMR12]. In short, this theorem states that
the level set of an image n0 by such function is a submanifold of M, and
its tangent space coincides with the kernel of the gradient. Here, we take
n0 = f ( x ) to make the connection with injectivity, and the theorem yields
that for all x, f −1 ( f ( x )) is a submanifold of M with

                Tx̃ f −1 ( f ( x )) = ker ∇ f ( x̃ )   ∀ x̃ ∈ f −1 ( f ( x )),    (5.2)

                                                                                 | 55
                      Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1

where Tx̃ M denotes the tangent space to manifold M at x̃.
    We first prove (a). Take x ∈ M, and assume (4.3). Each x̃ belonging to
a neighborhood of x in f −1 ( f ( x )) has the same ℓ-coordinate as x. There-
fore, any tangent vector to f −1 ( f ( x )) at x has a zero ℓ-component, and
the tangent space to this manifold at x is orthogonal to direction ℓ, i.e.
Tx f −1 ( f ( x )) ⊥ eℓ . Equation (5.2) evaluated at x̃ = x then gives (5.1). Since
this holds for any x ∈ M, the proof of (a) is complete.
    Now we prove (b). Assume (5.1) for all x, and consider a specific x.
Combining (5.1) with (5.2) yields Tx̃ f −1 ( f ( x )) ⊥ eℓ for all x̃ ∈ f −1 ( f ( x )).
Take a specific x̃ ∈ f −1 ( f ( x )), and assume that there is a path lying in
f −1 ( f ( x )) between x and x̃. At every point of this path, the tangent space
is orthogonal to direction ℓ, so the derivative along the path is zero in the ℓ-
coordinate. Integrating along the path gives a zero increase in ℓ-coordinate,
hence x̃ℓ = xℓ . This holds for all x ∈ M.
    To complete the proof, we need to show that there exists ϵ > 0 such that
for all x̃ ∈ B( x, ϵ) ∩ f −1 ( f ( x )), there is a path lying in f −1 ( f ( x )) between
x and x̃. Such path can be found with the rank theorem [AMR12]. Roughly
speaking, this theorem states that if ∇ f has constant rank k in a manifold
M, then dim M − k variables are redundant and can be eliminated. More
precisely, for all p in the manifold, there exist local coordinates centered at
p and f ( p) in which f (q) = (q1 , . . . , qk , 0, . . . , 0) for any q in the manifold.
     For the rest of this proof, we work in the local coordinates provided
by this theorem, centered at p = x. Since these are centered at x and f ( x ),
x1 = · · · = xk = 0. Let us now take ϵ > 0, and x̃ ∈ B( x, ϵ) ∩ f −1 ( f ( x )).
The rank theorem yields that the last entries of f ( x̃ ) are zero in those local
coordinates, i.e. f ( x̃ ) = ( x̃1 , . . . , x̃k , 0, . . . , 0). Since x̃ lies in the manifold
f −1 ( f ( x )), it must have the same image as x, hence f ( x̃ ) = f ( x ) = 0, and
x̃1 = · · · = x̃k = 0. An example of path between x and x̃ is given by
(0, . . . , 0, λ x̃k+1 , . . . , λ x̃dim M ), with λ ∈ [0, 1]. Applying the function f on
any point of this path gives 0 in the local coordinates by the rank theorem,
showing that the path is indeed included in the manifold f −1 ( f ( x )).

    Lemma 5.3 establishes the generic nature of the notion generic rank
introduced in Definition 3.1. For a concrete illustration of this concept, refer
to Example 3.2 and Fig. 3.1.

Lemma 5.3. For set M and naturals n1 , n2 , let M : M → Cn1 ×n2 be a matrix
analytic function, let r = grank ( M) ≜ maxx∈M rank M ( x ) be its generic rank

                                                                                       | 56
                  Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1

and q ∈ N such that 0 ≤ q < r. The set M is partitioned according to rank M( x )
as follows:

  (i) rank M ( x ) = r for all x ∈ M except possibly those lying on a closed set
      of dimension < dim M.
 (ii) rank M ( x ) = q for almost no x, i.e. only for x lying on a set of dimension
      < dim M.

Proof. Recall the following basic result of linear algebra [dW91]: the rank
of a matrix is q if it has a nonzero q-th order minor, while every (q + 1)-th
order minor is zero. The minor of a matrix being the determinant of one of
its submatrices, it is analytic in the entries of the matrix. Here, minors of
M( x ) are also analytic in x since f is analytic and composition preserves
analyticity. Since nonconstant analytic functions vanish only on a lower-
dimensional set [D’A10, p. 162], the zero set of each minor of M ( x ) is
lower-dimensional.
    First, we prove (i). As in [dW91], we define the set on which r is not
reached:

                     W<r = { x ∈ M | rank M( x ) < r }.

The above characterization of the rank yields

         W<r = { x ∈ M | each r-th order minor of M( x ) is zero},

which is the finite intersection of lower-dimensional sets, hence is lower-
dimensional. Besides, W<r is also closed: the inverse image of a closed set
by a continuous function is a closed set [Hau91], hence the zero sets of the
minors are closed, and so is their intersection.
   Next, we prove (ii). Let

                      Wq = { x ∈ M | rank M( x ) = q}.

Clearly, Wq ⊆ W<r . Since W<r is lower-dimensional, so is Wq .

    Building on the generic nature of the notion of generic rank, Lemma 5.4
establishes the pseudo-generic character of the orthogonality relation (5.3),
for any matrix analytic function M.

Lemma 5.4. For set M and naturals n1 , n2 , let M : M → Cn1 ×n2 be a matrix



                                                                            | 57
                        Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1

analytic function, and ℓ ∈ {1, . . . , dim M}. The orthogonality relation

                                      ker M ( x ) ⊥ eℓ                                    (5.3)

is pseudo-generic: it either holds

  (i) for almost all x ∈ M, i.e. for all x except possibly those lying on a set of
      dimension < dim M.
  (ii) for almost no x, i.e. only for x lying on a set of dimension < dim M.

Proof. Equation (5.3) can be equivalently rewritten as M ( x ) · δ = 0 ⇒ δℓ = 0
for all δ ∈ Cdim M , which is equivalent to the linear independence of the
ℓ-th column of M( x ) with the other columns, i.e.
                                                    
                           rank M ( x ) = rank M( x ) \ℓ + 1,                             (5.4)
             
where M ( x ) \ℓ denotes the matrix M( x ) without its ℓ-th column. Equation
(5.4) can be rewritten as

                i_
                 max                                             
                                                          
                         rank M ( x ) = i + 1 ∧ rank M( x ) \ℓ = i ,                      (5.5)
                 i =0

where imax ≜ min(dim M, n1 n2 ) − 1. For each i, both rank equalities of (5.5)
are pseudo-generic by Lemma 5.3. Then, equation (5.5) is pseudo-generic
by Lemma 2.5.

   Consider a parameterized function f , analytic in its variables and its
parameters. Applying Lemma 5.3 and Lemma 5.4 for M = ∇ f gives the
two following corollaries.

Corollary 5.5. For sets M◦ , M• , D f , N and M ≜ M◦ × M• \ D f , let f :
M → N be a function analytic in its variables x ◦ ∈ M◦ and in its parameters
x • ∈ M• , let r = max( x◦ ,x• )∈M rank ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) and q ∈ N such that 0 ≤
q < r. The set M is partitioned according to rank ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) as follows:

  (i) rank ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) = r for all ( x ◦ , x • ) ∈ M except possibly those lying on a
      closed set of dimension < dim M.
  (ii) rank ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) = q for almost no ( x ◦ , x • ), i.e. only for ( x ◦ , x • ) lying
       on a set of dimension < dim M.



                                                                                        | 58
                     Algebraic characterization of local injectivity | 5.1

Corollary 5.6. For sets M◦ , M• , D f , N and M ≜ M◦ × M• \ D f , let f :
M → N be a function analytic in its variables x ◦ ∈ M◦ and in its parameters
x • ∈ M• , and ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , dim M◦ }. The orthogonality relation

                                  ker ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) ⊥ eℓ                            (5.6)

is pseudo-generic: it either holds

  (i) for almost all ( x ◦ , x • ) ∈ M, i.e. for all ( x ◦ , x • ) except possibly those lying
      on a set of dimension < dim M.
  (ii) for almost no ( x ◦ , x • ), i.e. only for ( x ◦ , x • ) lying on a set of dimension
       < dim M.
   Proposition 5.7 characterizes local scalar-coordinate-injectivity almost
everywhere for analytic functions, building on Lemma 5.1, Corollary 5.5
and Corollary 5.6. Local scalar-coordinate-injectivity is closely related to
the orthogonality relation (5.6), hence is pseudo-generic: it either holds
almost everywhere, or almost nowhere. In Section 5.2, Proposition 5.7
                                                                     ◦
will be applied on h, defined in (4.2), with manifolds M◦ = Cm , M• =
   •
Cm , D f = Dh and N = CnB ·nC . Those manifolds could be defined more
specifically in Proposition 5.7, but we keep the statement in its most general
form.

Proposition 5.7. Let M◦ , M• be smooth, open manifolds, let D f be a closed
set, M ≜ M◦ × M• \ D f , N be a smooth manifold, and M or N has finite
dimension. Let f : M → N be a function analytic in its variables x ◦ ∈ M◦ and
in its parameters x • ∈ M• , and ℓ ∈ {1, 2, . . . , dim M◦ }. Exactly one of the two
following statements holds:

  (i) ker ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) ⊥ eℓ for almost all ( x ◦ , x • ) and f is locally scalar-
      coordinate-injective for ℓ at almost all ( x ◦ , x • ).
  (ii) For almost all ( x ◦ , x • ) : ker ∇ f ( x ◦ ; x • ) ̸⊥ eℓ and f is locally non-scalar-
       coordinate-injective for ℓ at ( x ◦ , x • ).

Proof. Let S1 ⊂ M be the set on which ∇ f does not reach its maximal rank,
and S2 ⊂ M the set on which (5.6) does not hold (case (i) of Corollary 5.6) or
holds (case (ii) of Corollary 5.6). Corollary 5.5 ensures that S1 is closed and
lower-dimensional, and Corollary 5.6 ensures that S2 is lower-dimensional.
We write cl S2 for the closure of S2 , i.e. the union of S2 and its boundary. Let
MS ≜ M \ (S1 ∪ cl S2 ). MS covers the whole domain M except a closed
lower-dimensional set, and it is open since M is assumed open.

                                                                                      | 59
                      Algebraic characterization of local identifiability | 5.2

          Thus, there is an ϵ for every ( p◦ , p• ) ∈ MS such that the rank of ∇ f is
      constant over Ball(( p◦ , p• ), ϵ), and (5.6) holds (case (i) of Corollary 5.6) or
      does not hold (case (ii) of Corollary 5.6) on the ball. Denote the restriction
      of f on Ball(( p◦ , p• ), ϵ), with fixed parameters p• by:

                      f ( p◦ ,p• ),ϵ : Ball(( p◦ , p• ), ϵ) → N : x ◦ → f ( x ◦ ; p• ).

      The result (i) follows by applying Lemma 5.1 (b) on f ( p◦ ,p• ),ϵ for all ( p◦ , p• ) ∈
      MS . Similarly, (ii) follows by applying the contraposition of Lemma 5.1 (a)
      on f ( p◦ ,p• ),ϵ for all ( p◦ , p• ) ∈ MS .

           Note the difference between the formulation of (i) and (ii). For (i), the set
      on which the orthogonality relation holds does not need to coincide with
      the set on which f is locally coordinate-injective. On the other hand, for
      (ii), the set on which the orthogonality relation does not hold is the same as
      the one were we have local non-coordinate-injectivity. This comes from the
      difference between (a) and (b) in Lemma 5.1.



5.2     Algebraic characterization of local identifiability

      In this section, we apply Proposition 5.7 to function h defined in (4.2). This
      leads to an algebraic characterization of local identifiability. We show that
      the local identifiability of a single unknown module is a pseudo-generic
      property, and that it becomes a generic property when the local identifiabil-
      ity of all unknown modules is considered.

      Definition 5.8. We define the matrix function K which, as will be proved in
      Lemma 5.10, is the gradient of function h introduced in (4.2).

      arranging vector of unknowns a◦ and knowns a• into the network structure
      of the problem, see Example 2.1 below. Symbol ⊗ denotes the Kronecker

    Combining the expression of ∇h derived in Lemma 5.10 with Proposi-
tion 5.7 yields the following algebraic necessary and sufficient characterization
for local identifiability: ker K ( a) ⊥ eℓ for almost all a if and only if Aij is


                                                                                       | 62
                Algebraic characterization of local identifiability | 5.2

locally identifiable at almost all a, where ℓ is the index of module Aij in
vector a◦ . Theorem 5.12 is a stronger version of this statement: it provides
characterization (ii) when (i) does not hold. Moreover, in (ii), the sets of a on
which the orthogonality relation does not hold, and local identifiability does
not hold, coincide exactly. The local indentifiability of an unknown module
is a pseudo-generic property: it either holds almost everywhere, or almost
nowhere. It follows from the pseudo-genericity of local scalar-coordinate-
injectivity, established in Proposition 5.7. Note that it is pseudo-generic in
both the unknown modules a◦ , and the known modules a• .

Theorem 5.12. Consider a network A = A◦ + A• with excitations matrix B, and
measurements matrix C. Consider the unknown module Aij◦ , let K ( a) be as defined
in (5.7), and let ℓ be the index of module Aij◦ in vector a◦ . Exactly one of the two
following statements holds:

  (i) ker K ( a) ⊥ eℓ for almost all a and Aij◦ is locally identifiable at almost all a.
  (ii) For almost all a : ker K ( a) ̸⊥ eℓ and Aij◦ is locally non-identifiable at a,

where a = ( a◦ , a• ) contains the unknown and known modules. Besides,
ker K ( a) ⊥ eℓ is equivalent to the following implication holding ∀ ∆◦ ∈ C L× L
with same zero entries as A◦ :

                    C T̄ ( a◦ ; a• ) ∆◦ T̄ ( a◦ ; a• ) B = 0 ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0.          (5.12)

   The proof of Theorem 5.12 relies on applying Proposition 5.7 on the
gradient of h, characterized by Lemma 5.10.

Proof. Lemma 4.16 establishes that the local identifiability of module Aij
is equivalent to the local scalar-coordinate-injectivity of function h defined
in (4.2) for coordinate ℓ. We verify the assumptions of Proposition 5.7 for
h. First, h is analytic, and its domain and image sets have finite dimension.
                                    ◦       •
The openness of the domain Cm × Cm \ Dh follows from the continuity
of det( I − Ā). Since the inverse image of a closed set by a continuous
function is a closed set [Hau91], the zero set of det( I − Ā) is closed, and its
complement is open. Its assumptions being fulfilled, Proposition 5.7 applies.
The first part of the theorem then follows directly from the expression of ∇h
given in (5.9). Besides, ker ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) ⊥ eℓ can be rewritten equivalently:

                            ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) δ = 0 ⇒ δℓ = 0.



                                                                                  | 63
                Algebraic characterization of local identifiability | 5.2

Reorganizing this n B · nC vector into a n B × nC matrix gives

                        mat ∇h( a◦ ; a• ) δ = 0 ⇒ δℓ = 0,
                                           


and combining it with (5.10) then yields (5.12).

   Building on Theorem 5.12, Proposition 5.13 establishes the equivalence
between global and local identifiability in case of full excitation (resp. mea-
surement).

Proposition 5.13. Consider a network A = A◦ + A• with all nodes excited, and
measurements matrix C (resp. all nodes measured, and excitations matrix B). For
                          ◦   •
all a = ( a◦ , a• ) ∈ Cm +m , each unknown module Aij◦ is globally identifiable at a
if and only if it is locally identifiable at a.

is globally identifiable at a = ( a◦ , a• ). Definition 4.14 states that for all

where ℓ is the index corresponding to Aij in vector a◦ . We pose ∆◦ ≜
