           C ( I − Ā − ∆◦ )−1 = C ( I − Ā)−1                        ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0
      ⇔ C = C ( I − Ā)−1 ( I − Ā − ∆◦ )                             ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0
      ⇔ C = C ( I − Ā)−1 ( I − Ā) − C ( I − Ā)−1 ∆◦                ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0
      ⇔ C = C · I − C ( I − Ā)−1 ∆◦                                  ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0
      ⇔ C ( I − Ā)−1 ∆◦ = 0                                          ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0
      ⇔ C ( I − Ā)−1 ∆◦ ( I − Ā)−1 B = 0                            ⇒ ∆ij◦ = 0,

the last line is obtained because ( I − Ā)−1 is invertible and B is the identity.
The last line matches equation (5.12), and is equivalent to generic local
identifiability at a. If all nodes are measured, then C = I and a similar
reasoning yields the same result.

  We saw in Theorem 5.12 that the local identifiability of an unknown
module is a pseudo-generic property. If we consider the local identifiability

                                                                                   | 64
                Algebraic characterization of local identifiability | 5.2

of a subset of the unknown modules, it is also pseudo-generic by Lemma 2.5:
any boolean combination of pseudo-generic properties is pseudo-generic.
But if we consider the local identifiability of all unknown modules, the
property becomes generic, as shown in Corollary 5.14. Hence we have the
following handy consequence of generic properties: if a network is locally
identifiable for one a0 , then it is locally identifiable for almost all a. We
have the following algebraic necessary and sufficient characterization for local
identifiability of all unknown modules: rank K ( a) = m◦ for almost all a if and
only if A◦ is locally identifiable at almost all a. Corollary 5.14 is a more
precise version of this statement: it provides characterization (ii) when (i)
does not hold.

Corollary 5.14. Consider a network A = A◦ + A• with excitations matrix B,
and measurements matrix C. Exactly one of the two following statements holds:

  (i) rank K ( a) = m◦ for almost all a and the network is locally identifiable at
      almost all a.
  (ii) rank K ( a) < m◦ for all a and the network is locally non-identifiable at all
       a,

where a = ( a◦ , a• ) contains the unknown and known modules. Besides,
rank K ( a) = m◦ is equivalent to the following implication holding ∀ ∆◦ ∈ C L× L
with same zero entries as A◦ :

                   C T̄ ( a◦ ; a• ) ∆◦ T̄ ( a◦ ; a• ) B = 0 ⇒ ∆◦ = 0.           (5.14)

   The proof of Corollary 5.14 relies on combining Theorem 5.12 for all
unknown modules.

Proof. First, ker K ( a) ⊥ eℓ for each ℓ is equivalent to ker K ( a) = {0}, and
therefore rank K ( a) = m◦ by the rank-nullity theorem. From Corollary 5.5,
the full rankness of K ( a) = ∇h( a◦ ) is not only pseudo-generic, but actually
generic: either (i) rank K ( a) = m◦ for almost all a, or (ii) rank K ( a) < m◦
for all a. In case (ii), for each a there is a direction ℓ for which ker K ( a) ̸⊥ eℓ ,
since the rank is non-full. Applying Theorem 5.12 (ii) for this a yields
the non-identifiability of the module corresponding to ℓ at a. We apply
this argument for each a, and obtain that in case (ii), the network is non-
identifiable for all a. Then, (5.12) for each unknown module (i, j) yields
(5.14).



                                                                                | 65
                         Algorithm to test generic local identifiability | 5.3

      Example 2.1 (continued). We have rank K ( a) = 2 for all a except on
       ◦ ◦ • •
       ( a1 , a2 , a1 , a2 ) ∈ C4 | a1• = 0 , which is a set of complex dimension 3. From
      Corollary 5.14, the network is locally identifiable at almost all a.
         We observe that condition (5.14) reduces to the necessary and sufficient
      condition for generic global identifiability in the full excitation (resp. mea-
      surement) case developed in [HGB18]. Indeed, since T̄ = ( I − Ā)−1 is by
      construction invertible, C T̄∆◦ T̄B = 0 is equivalent to C T̄∆◦ = 0 when B is
      the identity matrix (resp. to ∆◦ T̄B when C is the identity matrix), which
      leads to Theorem 3.6 (resp. Theorem 3.7) in [HGB18].



5.3     Algorithm to test generic local identifiability

      The necessary and sufficient conditions of Theorem 5.12 can be tested ex-
      actly by symbolic computation, but it becomes rapidly computationally
      intractable. Hence, we have designed a probability-1 algorithm that ex-
      ploits the pseudo-generic character of local identifiability, as established
      in Theorem 5.12. Testing these conditions on a randomly selected matrix
      A with the network structure will indeed provide the correct result with a
      probability 1, and can be done very efficiently. The matrix A is generated
      randomly in the following manner: each entry corresponding to an edge is
      independently drawn from the uniform distribution on the interval (0, 1).
      This extends the work of E. J. Davison in the controllability context [Dav77].
          Although this idea allows to design an algorithm working with proba-
      bility 1, an actual implementation could suffer from numerical issues for
      large networks, e.g. if the randomly selected values are close to the prob-
      lematic lower-dimensional set. To increase the reliability of our results, the
      procedure is repeated nsamples times, where nsamples denotes the number
      of independent trials performed, as described in Algorithm 2. However, we
      did not observe any numerical issues in our sanity checks, as detailed below.
      For small networks, where generic local identifiability could be determined
      manually, the algorithm consistently produced results identical to our man-
      ual analysis. For larger networks of O(102 ) nodes, we verified robustness
      by performing the tests multiple times and confirming that each sample
      yielded identical outcomes. Across the 106 tests conducted, no numerical
      problems were detected. Although no numerical issues were observed dur-
      ing our sanity checks, we recommend using a value of nsamples = 10 for


                                                                                  | 66
                   Algorithm to test generic local identifiability | 5.3

networks of less than 500 nodes, to ensure robustness. For larger-scale net-
works, where computational cost becomes more significant, we recommend
smaller values such as nsamples = 1 or 2.
    We know from Corollary 5.14 that the local identifiability of the whole
network is a generic property: if there is a sample for which matrix K has
full column rank, then it has full column rank almost everywhere, and the
network is generically locally identifiable (line 6). If the whole network is
not generically locally identifiable, then the algorithm returns the generic
local identifiability of each module. Theorem 5.12 ensures that the local
identifiability of a single module (or a subset of the unknown modules) is
pseudo-generic: it holds either almost everywhere, or almost nowhere. In
order to compute the value taken by most samples, we average the values
taken by all samples and round the result (lines 11-12). Our implementation
is available at our GitHub repository [LH25], and includes a graphical
representation of the results.
    At this stage, the reader might understandably be confused by the
simultaneous use of the terms generic and pseudo-generic. We therefore
provide the following clarifications. Algorithm 2 is designed to determine
the generic local identifiability of the unknown modules, as formally defined
in Definition 4.15. In order to do so, the algorithm relies on its pseudo-generic
character, as established in Theorem 5.12.
    Regarding time complexity, the bottlenecks are the computation of
rank K at line 5 and the computation of the basis of ker K at line 7:
O(n B · nC · m◦ · max(n B nC , m◦ )) for both, and experiments show that com-
puting the basis of ker K is the most costly operation. Algorithm 2 typically
takes 3 seconds per sample for an Erdős–Rényi network of 1000 nodes,
1500 modules among which 500 are unknown, 300 excitations and 300
measurements.
    Fig. 5.1 shows examples of outputs of Algorithm 2. Fig. 5.1a (left) shows
a nontrivial example of network where only one module is generically lo-
cally identifiable. Interestingly, switching excitation/measurement between
nodes 4 and 5 allows to recover the identifiability of all unknown modules,
see Fig. 5.1a (right). This result is not trivial by inspection, but is computed
instantaneously by Algorithm 2.
    Fig. 5.1b is the example of Section VII in [DSC+ 22], for which the authors
provide an excitation set guaranteeing the generic global identifiability of
all unknown modules, assuming that all nodes are measured. Algorithm 2

                                                                          | 67
                     Algorithm to test generic local identifiability | 5.3




Algorithm 2 Generic local identifiability test
Require: The unknown and known edge topology, matrices B and C,
      nsamples
Result: The generic local identifiability of each unknown module

 1: Initialize modules to a vector of 0’s of size m◦
      (1 := generically locally identifiable, 0 := not generically locally identif.)
 2: repeat nsamples times:
 3:      Randomly generate A, a network matrix with nonzero structure
      dictated by the graph topology. Each nonzero entry of A is drawn
      independently from U (0, 1).
         Build K = B⊤ T ⊤ ⊗ CT I ◦ , where T = ( I − A)−1
                                 
 4:
 5:      if rank K = m◦ then
 6:          return “Network generically locally identifiable”
 7:      else Compute V, basis of ker K
 8:          Calculate v, a binary vector of size m◦ , with
 9:          vi = 1 if i-th entry of each vector of V is 0
10:             = 0 otherwise
11:          modules ← modules + v/nsamples
12: return round(modules)

5.4     Summary

      To summarize the results of this chapter, we have derived algebraic neces-
      sary and sufficient conditions for generic local identifiability (i) for a single
      unknown module: Theorem 5.12, (ii) for all unknown modules: Corollary
      5.14. We have established that local identifiability is a pseudo-generic prop-
      erty. Moreover, when all unknown modules are considered jointly, this
      property strengthens and becomes generic. These conditions can be easily
      checked numerically by Algorithm 2, implemented at [LH25].
             These algebraic conditions allow rapidly testing generic local identi-

                                                                                   | 69
                                                          Summary | 5.4

fiability for any given network, but do not provide any insight on the
dynamics occurring in the network. We wish to find a combinatorial char-
acterization for generic identifiability, that is expressed purely in terms of
graph-theoretical properties, akin to what was done in the full excitation
case e.g. in [HGB18]. Such characterization would in particular pave the
way for optimizing the selection of nodes to be excited and measured, akin
to the works in the full measurement case [CSVdH21, DSC+ 22].




                                                                       | 70
             Coupled, decoupled and
                                                                   6
               separable Excited and
                 Measured networks


T
        HEOREM 5.12 gives an algebraic characterization for generic local
        identifiability, that enables rapid testing with Algorithm 2. However,
        this characterization does not provide any understanding on the
dynamics occurring in the network. In this chapter, we show how graphical
insight can be obtained from equation (5.12). A quadruple ( A◦ , A• , B, C ) is
called an EM network, for Excited and Measured network.
    First, we show that equation (5.12) can be expressed as the global identi-
fiability of an associated EM network, called the coupled EM network. The
coupled EM network is obtained by duplicating the initial graph, applying
the excitations on one copy, taking the measurements on the other copy and
adding the unknown modules between the two copies, from the excited
copy to the measured one. All the other modules, inside each copy, are
known, and are coupled: each module of one copy must be equal to its corre-
sponding module in the other copy. We will establish in Proposition 6.6 that
generic local identifiability is equivalent to generic global identifiability of
the associated coupled EM network.
    The coupling constraint in the coupled EM network poses difficulties:
it lacks a physical interpretation and is challenging to formalize. Relax-

                                                                         | 71
                                                                             | 6.0

ing these coupling relations gives birth to the decoupled EM network. The
decoupled EM network is simply the coupled EM network with the cou-
pling relations removed. The generic global identifiability of the decoupled
network is denoted as generic decoupled identifiability. By construction,
generic decoupled identifiability is necessary for generic local identifiability.
    Allowing the two subgraphs of the decoupled EM network to have
different topologies gives rise to the class of separable EM networks. Separable
EM networks generalize decoupled networks and possess the appealing
property that generic global identifiability is equivalent to generic local
identifiability. This property enables to address generic global identifiability
with the algebraic tools of Chapter 5.

    Generic global identifiability
                 ⇓
    Generic local identifiability            ⇔      Generic global identifiability
                                                    of the coupled EM network
               ⇓1
 Generic decoupled identifiability           ⇔     Generic global identifiability
                                                   of the decoupled EM network

                                                  Separable EM networks
                                Two subgraphs, unknown modules linking them

                                          Decoupled EM networks
                                The two subgraphs have same topology

                                 Coupled EM networks
                          The known modules are coupled

6.1     The coupled and decoupled EM networks

      Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), with A = A◦ + A• : its coupled
      network is obtained by duplicating A, applying the excitations on one
      copy, taking the measurements on the other copy and adding the unknown
      modules between the two copies, from the excited copy to the measured
      one. All the other modules, inside each copy, are known, and are coupled:
      each module of one copy must be equal to its corresponding module in the
      other copy. A formal definition is given below and it is illustrated in Fig.
      6.1. The coupled network of Example 2.1 is in Fig. 6.2.
      Definition 6.1. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ) of L nodes, with
      A = A◦ + A• . Its coupled EM network, ( Ǎ◦ , Ǎ• , B̌, Č ) is comprised of 2L
      nodes {1, . . . , L, 1′ , . . . , L′ }, and its network matrices are as follows

      Note 6.2. Each unknown modules of the initial EM network A gives birth to
      three modules in its coupled EM network: a known module in the excited
      copy, a known module in the measured copy, which is equal to the one in
      the excited copy, and an unknown module between the two copies.
          The decoupled EM network is a relaxed variant of the coupled EM
      network, in which the modules in the excited and measured subgraphs are
      allowed to differ, while the topology of the two subgraphs remains identical.
      A formal definition is given below and it is illustrated in Fig. 6.1.
      Definition 6.3. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ) of L nodes, with
      A = A◦ + A• . Its decoupled EM network, ( Â◦ , Â• , B̂, Ĉ ) is comprised of 2L
      nodes {1, . . . , L, 1′ , . . . , L′ }, and its network matrices are as follows

6.2     Separable networks

      We now introduce separable EM networks, which are a generalization of the
      decoupled EM network, where the excited and measured subgraphs are
      not required to have the same topology anymore. Our focus on separable
      EM networks is motivated by two considerations. First, every decoupled
      EM network is, by definition, separable. Second, despite attempts, we
      were not able to fully exploit the additional structure of decoupled EM
      networks in our analysis. For these reasons, it is natural to conduct the
      analysis in this section for the more general class of separable EM networks.
      Alternatively, separable EM networks can be defined as a class of EM
      networks for which excitations and measurements can be isolated in two
      distinct subgraphs, with no known module linking the two subgraphs.
      Between the two subgraphs lie all the unknown modules, going from the
      excited subgraph A B to the measured one AC . A formal definition in terms
      of network matrices is given in Definition 6.4, and an example is given in
      Fig. 6.3.


                             AB           A⊙            AC




      Fig. 6.3 An example of separable EM network: the excitations (dotted
      purple) are isolated in a subgraph where all modules are known, and
      the measurements (dotted blue) in another subgraph of known modules.
      The unknown modules (dashed orange) link the excited subgraph to the
      measured one. One can check that this EM network is generically globally
      identifiable.


      Definition 6.4. A separable EM network is an EM network which nodes can
      be separated in two sets of nodes of size L B and LC , such that the network

                                                          Separable networks | 6.2

matrices have the following block structure:
                                                     ⋄

      A⊙          0           0           AC             0

where A⊙ ∈ C LC × LB , A B ∈ C LB × LB , AC ∈ C LC × LC , B⋄ ∈ C LB ×nB and
C ⋄ ∈ Cn C × L C .

   We have the following important property: on separable EM networks,
global and local identifiability are equivalent generically.

unknown modules Aij◦ is generically locally identifiable if and only if it is generically
globally identifiable.

6.3     Identifiability of the coupled and decoupled networks

      In Proposition 6.6, we establish that generically, the global identifiability
      of the coupled network encodes precisely the algebraic characterization of
      local identifiability of Theorem 5.12.
      Proposition 6.6. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). An unknown module
      Aij◦ is generically locally identifiable if and only if in its associated coupled EM
      network ( Ǎ◦ , Ǎ• , B̌, Č ), the corresponding unknown module Ǎi◦′ j is generically
      globally identifiable.
      Proof. Since the coupled network is a particular case of separable network,
      Proposition 6.5 assures that the generic global identifiability of its unknown
      modules is equivalent to their generic local identifiability. Then, formulating
      the algebraic necessary an sufficient condition of Theorem 5.12 for Ǎi′ j , and
      developing from the block structure of (6.1) in the same fashion as the proof
      of Proposition 6.5 yields precisely (5.12) for Aij , which is equivalent to its
      generic local identifiability.

         We introduce the notion of decoupled identifiability, which will be proved
      equivalent to the global identifiability of the decoupled EM network, when
      considered generically.

where vec stacks the columns of its L × L matrix argument into a L2 vector,
see Example 4.2. Then, observing that vec (∆◦ ) = I ◦ δ yields
                                                                                  ◦
                             K̂ ( a B , aC ) δ = 0 ⇒ δℓ = 0          ∀ δ ∈ Cm ,

which is equivalent to the orthogonality relation (6.7). Then, applying
Lemma 5.4 to K̂ establishes the pseudo-generic character of (6.7).

    When all unknown modules are considered jointly, decoupled identifia-
bility becomes a generic property, as shown in Corollary 6.11.

Corollary 6.11. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). The network is

  (i) either rank K̂ ( a B , aC ) = m◦ for almost all ( a B , aC ),
  (ii) or rank K̂ ( a B , aC ) < m◦ for all ( a B , aC ).

      Proof. Combining the result of Proposition 6.10 for each unknown module
      as in the proof of Corollary 5.14 yields (6.8), which is generic by Lemma 5.3.




6.5     Decoupled identifiability and local identifiability

      When the identifiability of all unknown modules is considered jointly, the
      notion of generic decoupled identifiability introduced in Definition 6.7 is
      necessary for generic local identifiability.
      Proposition 6.12. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). If the network is
      generically locally identifiable, then it is generically decoupled-identifiable.
      Proof. Take ( a◦ , a• ), at which the whole network A◦ is locally identifiable.
      Equation (5.14) holds, and it is decoupled-identifiable at ( a◦B = a◦ , a•B =

      a subset of the unknown modules, since their local identifiability is only a
      pseudo-generic property, not a generic one, see Proposition 6.10.

 generate two matrices A B and AC with same nonzero structure, dictated by
 the graph topology, and compute K̂ = B⊤ ( I − A B )−⊤ ⊗ C ( I − AC )−1 I ◦ .
                                                                            

 Calculating the rank of K̂ then yields the generic decoupled identifiability of
 the network, and a basis of ker K̂ gives the generic decoupled identifiability
 of each unknown module.
     There is a feature for the decoupled network in our implementation
 [LH25]. When the user opts for decoupled identifiability, the toolbox displays
 a graphical representation of both the initial network, and its decoupled
 network. And we know from Proposition 6.9 that a network is generically
 decoupled-identifiable if and only if its decoupled network is generically
 globally identifiable. An example of output is given in Fig. 6.4, and our
 toolbox is available at [LH25].
     Proposition 6.12 establishes the necessity of generic decoupled iden-
 tifiability of the entire network for generic local identifiability of the en-
 tire network. Regarding the potential sufficiency: no EM network that is
 generically decoupled-identifiable, but not generically locally identifiable is
 known to the authors, despite numerous automatic tests. In other words,
 experiments seem to show that when the identifiability of all unknown mod-
 ules is considered jointly, generic decoupled identifiability is equivalent to
 generic local identifiability.

                                                                                 | 81
                                                Contribution of this chapter | 6.6

      Conjecture 6.14. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). The network is
      generically locally identifiable if and only if it is generically decoupled-identifiable.



6.6     Contribution of this chapter

      This chapter served primarily as an exploratory study. While it did not
      directly yield many actionable results for the main research problem, it
      provided valuable insights and a deeper understanding of the algebraic
      conditions developed in Chapter 5.
          In particular, this chapter contributed to a better interpretation of Theo-
      rem 5.12 through the notion of the coupled EM network. We established,
      in Proposition 6.6, that generic local identifiability is equivalent to generic
      global identifiability of the associated coupled EM network.
          Furthermore, by relaxing the coupling constraints, we introduced the
      concept of the decoupled EM network, which naturally led to the definition
      of separable EM networks. These separable networks have the appealing
      property that generic global identifiability coincides with generic local
      identifiability. This property opens the door to addressing generic global
      identifiability using the algebraic tools developed in Chapter 5.




                                                                                        | 82
        PART II
   Combinatorial and
walk-based conditions for
      identifiability
                  Combinatorial
                                                                 7
    characterization of network
                   identifiability


T
        HROUGHOUT the remainder of this thesis, starting from this chapter,
        we focus on the identifiability of all unknown modules simultane-
        ously. This property is commonly referred to as the identifiability of
the entire network, or simply network identifiability.
    Corollary 5.14 provides an algebraic characterization of generic local
network identifiability, formulated in terms of the rank of a matrix K, as
defined in Definition 5.8. However, this algebraic condition offers limited
insight into the structural or dynamical behavior of the network.
    To bridge this gap, this chapter develops a combinatorial characteriza-
tion that translates the rank condition of Corollary 5.14 into network-level
combinatorial terms. This reveals how network topology and walk-based
patterns underpin the identifiability of its modules. This combinatorial
perspective lays the foundation for the constructive results and algorithmic
methods presented in the subsequent chapters.
   This chapter is based on [LH24] and its preliminary version [LH23].




                                                                       | 84
                                 Identifiability as matrix nonsingularity | 7.1


7.1     Identifiability as matrix nonsingularity

      A known result from linear algebra is that the rank of a matrix is the size
      of its largest square submatrix with nonzero determinant [HJ12]. Corol-
      lary 5.14 can then be rewritten in terms of determinant.

      Corollary 7.1. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). Exactly one of the two
      following statements holds:

        (i) The network is locally identifiable at almost all a, and there is a m◦ × m◦
            submatrix of K ( a), denoted KP ( a), such that det KP ( a) ̸= 0 for almost all
            a;
       (ii) The network is locally non-identifiable at all a, and for each m◦ × m◦ sub-
            matrix of K ( a), denoted KP ( a), there holds det KP ( a) = 0 for all a.

          Matrix K, defined in Definition 5.8, is of dimensions n B nC × m◦ . Hence,
      extracting the submatrix KP amounts to selecting m◦ rows of K. By con-
      struction of K, each of its rows corresponds to an excitation-measurement
      pair. We denote P ⊆ B × C the set of pairs selected by KP . This is formally
      introduced in Definition 7.2, and illustrated in Example 7.3.

      Definition 7.2. Consider an EM network with m◦ unknown modules, ex-
      citation set B and measurement set C . The notation P denotes a set of
      excitation-measurement pairs (denoted EM pairs) of cardinality m◦ :

                         P ⊆ B × C,                        |P | = m◦ .                (7.1)

      Notation KP refers to the submatrix of K obtained by selecting the rows
      corresponding to P . Since |P | = m◦ , KP is square. We also write
           n                           o               n                           o
      B P ≜ b ∈ B | ∃c ∈ C : (b, c) ∈ P ,         C P ≜ c ∈ C | ∃b ∈ B : (b, c) ∈ P ,
           n                  o                        n                  o
      BcP ≜ b ∈ B | (b, c) ∈ P ,                  CbP ≜ c ∈ C | (b, c) ∈ P ,

      nP      P
       B c ≜ Bc ,                                nP      P
                                                  C b ≜ Cb .                          (7.2)

      Example 7.3. Consider the EM network shown in Fig. 7.1. We have
                 n                         o
              P = (1, 14), (2, 13), (2, 14) ⊂ B × C = {1, 2} × {13, 14},

          nP

                             B 14 = 2,           nP
                                                  C 1 = 1,          nP
                                                                     C 2 = 2.

         If there are exactly n B nC unknown modules, i.e. as many as the number
      of EM pairs, then KP = K and P = B × C . If there are more unknown
      modules, then the network is not identifiable since there are more unknowns
      than equations available.



7.2     Taylor expansion in the graph

      Our combinatorial characterization will be derived from the expansion
      of det KP . By definition, the entries of the matrix KP are functions of the
      matrix T ( A). In this section, we express the entries of T ( A) explicitly in
      terms of the modules Aij . These expressions will serve as the foundation
      for the combinatorial characterization developed in the following sections.
          The analytic matrix T ( A) ≜ ( I − A)−1 can be expanded in the Taylor
      series
                                                             ∞
                             T ( A ) = ( I − A ) −1 = I + ∑ A k ,                     (7.3)
                                                         k =1

      which converges if |λi | < 1 for each eigenvalue λi of A = Ā( a◦ , a• ), with
      a = ( a◦ , a• ). For ϵ sufficiently small, this is true for a ∈ Ball(0, ϵ), for any
      network topology. Indeed, consider first the case a = 0. Then A = 0, and
      all eigenvalues of A are equal to zero. Since the eigenvalues of a matrix
      depend continuously on its entries [HJ12], there exists ϵ > 0 such that for
      all a ∈ Ball(0, ϵ), the spectral radius ρ( A) remains strictly less than one.
      We assume this holds for the rest of this chapter, and since Theorem 7.9
      characterizes a generic property, the fact that it holds on Ball(0, ϵ) implies
      that it holds almost everywhere.
                                                       h i

7.3     Definitions on the graph

      Definitions 7.5, 7.6 and 7.7 introduce notions for developing our combina-
      torial characterization, and Example 7.8 illustrates those notions. Those
      definitions assume a fixed set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ . In
      the sequel we denote unknown module Aij◦ with letter α to lighten notations.
      Definition 7.5. An assignation σ is a function that assigns a pair excitation-
      measurement to each unknown module:
                                                           
                          σ : E ◦ → P : α → σB (α), σC (α) ,                   (7.6)


                                                                                           | 87
                                               Definitions on the graph | 7.3

where σB (α) is the excitation assigned to α, σC (α) is its measurement. ΣP is
the set of all such bijective assignations, i.e. those for which each EM pair of
P is assigned to one and only one unknown module.
    Consider a bijective assignation σ1 ∈ ΣP . Swapping the images of
two elements, i.e., exchanging σ1 (αi ) and σ1 (α j ), yields another bijective
assignation σ2 ∈ ΣP . Such an operation is called a transposition. Fix an
arbitrary bijective assignation σP ref ∈ ΣP as a reference. Any assignation
σ ∈ ΣP can be obtained from σP ref through a sequence of transpositions.
Although the specific sequence is not unique, the number of transpositions
used always has the same parity — either always even or always odd —
regardless of the decomposition [DF+ 04]. The signature function

                                sgn : ΣP → {−1, 1}                                  (7.7)

equals +1 if the number of transpositions used to obtain the considered
assignation is even, and −1 otherwise.

Definition 7.6. A collection of walks ω is a set of m◦ walks ωi : one ωi for each
unknown module αi . Each walk ωi is a sequence of connected edges that
starts at an excited node b, goes through unknown module αi , and ends at a
measured node c, such that (b, c) ∈ P . Its B-part is the subsequence between
b and s(αi ), and its C-part is the subsequence between t(αi ) and c. On
separable EM networks, each ωi goes through one and only one unknown
module αi . There can be several different collections of walks corresponding
to the same assignation. Ωσ is the set of all ω corresponding to assignation
σ, and sgn(ω ) is the signature of the assignation corresponding to ω, as
defined in (7.7).
We say that ω is bijective if no two walks of ω start at the same excitation
and end at the same measurement — equivalently, the assignation σ corre-
sponding to ω is bijective. ΩP is the set of all bijective collection of walks ω
with set of EM pairs P .

Definition 7.7. Consider a collection of walks ω. Its monomial µ(ω ) is
the cumulative product for all unknown modules αi , of the product of all
modules taken by the corresponding walk ωi , except αi itself:

          µ(ω ) ≜    ∏ AωC (αi ),∗ · · · A∗,t(αi ) · As(αi ),∗ · · · A∗,ωB (αi )    (7.8)
                    αi ∈E ◦

where ωC (αi ) and ω B (αi ) are respectively the end and start nodes of walk

                                                                                   | 88
                                                    Definitions on the graph | 7.4

ωi , t(αi ) and s(αi ) are respectively the end and start nodes of module αi ,
and each module Aij may appear several times in µ(ω ), which would mean
that it is taken several times by ω. On separable EM networks, due to their
particular structure, each monomial is comprised of only known modules,
and no unknown one.
MP is the set of all monomials µ corresponding to bijective collections of
walks with set of EM pairs P , and Ωµ is the set of all bijective collections of
walks ω corresponding to monomial µ.
We define the repetition of a monomial as the aggregate of the signatures of
all bijective ω’s corresponding to that monomial.

                                 r (µ) ≜     ∑ sgn(ω ),                       (7.9)
                                            ω ∈Ωµ


where sgn(ω ) is the signature of the assignation corresponding to ω, as
defined in (7.7).

represents a special case: a separable EM network. While this choice helps
illustrate certain properties of ω and µ that are specific to separable EM
networks, it is important to emphasize that Definitions 7.5, 7.6 and 7.7 apply
to EM networks of arbitrary topology, not just the separable ones. Fig. 7.1
(a) represents the following collection of walks:

7.4     Determinant as sum of monomials

      With Corollary 7.1, the expression of Tij ( A) from Lemma 7.4, and the graph-
      theoretic definitions introduced in Section 7.3, we are now equipped to
      derive our combinatorial characterization from the expression of the matrix
      K as defined in Definition 5.8. We consider a fixed set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C
      with cardinality m◦ , such that the corresponding submatrix KP is square.
          We develop det KP as the sum over all possible row-column permuta-
      tions by Leibniz formula:

                     det KP =     ∑ sgn(σ) ∏◦ TσC (α),t(α) Ts(α),σB (α)                           (7.11)
                                 σ ∈ΣP               α∈E

      where s(α) and t(α) are respectively the start and end nodes of edge α, and
      each row-column permutation corresponds to a bijective assignation σ ∈ ΣP ,
      introduced in Definition 7.5.
         The Leibniz formula can be further developed by substituting (7.5) in the
      equation (7.11), thereby making the network modules Aij appear explicitly:
                                               "                                            
              det KP =    ∑ sgn(σ) ∏◦                        ∑     AσC (α),∗ · · · A∗,t(α)
                         σ ∈ΣP           α∈E               all walks
                                                         t(α)→σC (α)
                                                                                            #
                                                 ·           ∑    As(α),∗ · · · A∗,σB (α)
                                                          all walks
                                                         σB (α)→s(α)

      Distributing the product over all unknown modules α gives the monomials
      of all bijective collections of walks corresponding to the bijective assignation
      σ, i.e. all ω ∈ Ωσ (see Definition 7.6 for more details about those notions):

                            det KP =      ∑ sgn(σ) ∑ µ(ω ),                                       (7.12)
                                         σ ∈ΣP                 ω ∈Ωσ

      where µ(ω ) is the monomial corresponding to ω, introduced in Definition
      7.7. From (7.12), we group by monomials of same ω: instead of summing




                                                                                                  | 91
                                           Combinatorial characterization | 7.5

      over σ and then over ω, we sum directly over ω:

                               det KP =      ∑ sgn(ω ) µ(ω ),                       (7.13)
                                           ω ∈ΩP

      where ΩP is the set of all bijective collections of walks ω, and sgn(ω ) is the
      signature of the assignation corresponding to ω.
          Observe that from a same group of modules, it may be possible to build
      different bijective ω (see the three ω’s of Fig. 7.1), such that these different
      ω’s have the same monomial µ. Regrouping the same monomials together
      yields
                                                               
                            det KP =     ∑          ∑ sgn(ω )       µ,              (7.14)
                                        µ∈MP       ω ∈Ωµ


      where MP and Ωµ are introduced in Definition 7.7. Equation (7.14) can be
      rewritten concisely using the repetition r (µ) defined in (7.9):

                                   det KP =        ∑ r(µ) µ.                        (7.15)
                                               µ∈MP

         Equation (7.15) plays a central role in our analysis. As established in
      Corollary 7.1, generic local network identifiability is characterized by the
      determinants of square submatrices of K, denoted KP . Equation (7.13)
      expresses det KP as a sum over all bijective collections of walks ω, each
      weighted by its signature. Grouping together all ω corresponding to the
      same monomial µ leads to the more structured form in Equation (7.15).



7.5     Combinatorial characterization

      The repetition r (µ) accounts for all ω corresponding to monomial µ, and
      plays a crucial role in the combinatorial characterization below. By combin-
      ing Equation (7.15) with Corollary 7.1, we obtain a necessary and sufficient
      combinatorial condition for generic local identifiability of the entire network,
      formulated in terms of r (µ).

      Theorem 7.9. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). The network is generi-
      cally locally identifiable if and only if there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of


                                                                                    | 92
                                   Combinatorial characterization | 7.5

cardinality m◦ such that the associated set of monomials MP contains at least one
monomial µ with nonzero repetition, i.e., r (µ) ̸= 0.

Proof. From Corollary 7.1, we know that A◦ is generically locally identifiable
if and only if there is a m◦ × m◦ submatrix of K ( a), denoted KP ( a), such
that det KP ( a) ̸= 0 for almost all a. Selecting one such submatrix KP
amounts to selecting a subset of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ .
Equation (7.15) expresses det KP as a sum over all monomials µ of the
set MP , weighted by their repetition r (µ). A sum of distinct monomials
is generically nonzero if and only if at least one of the monomials has a
nonzero coefficient. Therefore, for this sum to be nonzero for almost all a, at
least one µ must have a nonzero repetition r (µ).

Example 7.8 (continued). Consider the EM network depicted in Fig. 7.1,
and the monomial µ expressed in (7.10). Its set Ωµ is comprised of three
bijective collections of walks, represented in Fig. 7.1, we denote them
ω a , ω b , ω c respectively. Fix σP ref as the assignation corresponding to ω a .
We have

  r (µ) =    ∑ sgn(ω ) = sgn(ω a ) + sgn(ω b ) + sgn(ω c ) = 1 − 1 + 1 = 1,
            ω ∈Ωµ


and Theorem 7.9 ensures that this network is generically locally identifiable.
    Theorem 7.9 provides a necessary and sufficient combinatorial char-
acterization of generic local network identifiability, expressed in terms of
monomial repetitions in the graph. These monomials represent collections
of walks, and their repetition aggregates their associated signatures. While
this characterization is exact, it is not directly exploitable for algorithmic
purposes due to its combinatorial complexity. The subsequent chapters
aim to extract more practical, tractable conditions from this framework,
ultimately leading to algorithmic methods developed in Part III.




                                                                           | 93
                 Walk-based conditions
                                                                  8
B
       UILDING on the combinatorial framework established in Chapter 7,
       this chapter aims to translate the abstract characterization of iden-
       tifiability into more tangible, graph-theoretic conditions.
   We begin by deriving direct consequences of Theorem 7.9, which lay
the groundwork for the more refined results that follow. To deepen those
results, we then introduce a decomposition of the assignation function σ,
enabling a finer analysis of the algebraic characterization of Chapter 5.
    This refinement leads to the central result of the chapter: Theorem 8.7,
which extends earlier results from the full excitation (respectively, mea-
surement) case [HGB18]. The theorem provides a necessary condition for
identifiability based on the existence of vertex-disjoint walks, along with
a sufficient condition of similar flavor. The proof of Theorem 8.7 relies on
two key lemmas. By dissecting the structure of σ at a more granular level,
these lemmas reveal the presence of vertex-disjoint walk patterns that arise
naturally from the algebraic framework.
     The structural insight offered by Theorem 8.7 gives rise to a conjecture
suggesting a possible strengthening of the result. Although this conjecture
appears plausible, it is disproven by a counterexample. This refutation not
only clarifies the limitations of the current approach but also underscores
the need for deeper combinatorial insight to fully resolve the network iden-
tifiability question in the general case of partial excitation and measurement.
This chapter is based on [LH21a, LH21b].

                                                                        | 94
                                    Conditions with collections of walks | 8.2


8.1     Conditions with collections of walks

      A first set of conditions can be directly extracted from Theorem 7.9. These
      include a necessary condition and a sufficient one, formulated in terms of
      the existence and uniqueness of bijective collections of walks. Together, they
      form the conceptual basis for the analysis developed in this chapter and
      will be refined in the sections that follow.

      Proposition 8.1. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). We have the two
      following conditions for generic local network identifiability:

        (i) If the network is generically locally identifiable, then there exists a set of EM
            pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ such that there is at least one bijective
            collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP , i.e. |ΩP | ≥ 1.
        (ii) If there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ such that there
             is only one bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP , i.e. |ΩP | = 1, then the
             network is generically locally identifiable.

      Proof. (i) Assume that A◦ is generically locally identifiable. Then, from
      Theorem 7.9, there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ such
      that the associated set of monomials MP contains at least one monomial µ
      with nonzero repetition, i.e., r (µ) ̸= 0. Hence Ωµ ̸= ∅, i.e. there is at least
      one bijective collection of walks ω corresponding to µ.
          (ii) Assume there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦
      such that there is only one bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP . Consider
      its corresponding monomial µ. We have r (µ) = ±1, and the network is
      generically locally identifiable by Theorem 7.9.




8.2     Decomposition of an assignation

      This section introduces and illustrates key notions that will be essential for
      the developments in Section 8.3. We begin by showing how the assignation
      function σ can be decomposed into two components: σB , which handles the
      assignation of excitations, and σC , which handles that of measurements.
          We then introduce terminology to describe whether a pair (σB , σC ) con-
      stitutes a bijective assignation and whether it is compatible with a given set

                                                                                      | 95
                                   Decomposition of an assignation | 8.2

P . Furthermore, we show how σC can be decomposed into sub-assignations
σCb , one for each excitation, and how their respective signatures are related.
   We acknowledge that the definitions introduced in this section are some-
what heavy and technical. To facilitate their understanding, we provide a
concrete illustration in Example 8.3 and Fig. 8.1.

Definition 8.2. Consider a fixed set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ .
The assignation function σ introduced in Definition 7.5 can be decomposed
into a B-assignation and a C-assignation:

                σB : E ◦ → B P ,                σC : E ◦ → C P .            (8.1)

   • σB (resp. σC ) is connected if for each unknown module α, there is a
     walk from its assigned excitation σB (α) to its start node s(α) (resp.
     from its end node t(α) to its assigned measurement σC (α)).

   • σB and σC are said bijection-pairable if the resulting assignation σ =
     (σB , σC ) is bijective.

   • σB and σC are said P -compatible if the resulting assignation σ =
     (σB , σC ) only assigns EM pairs that belong to P , i.e. Im σ = P .
       Pσ
   • ΣC B is the set of all σC bijection-pairable and P -compatible with σB .

   • σB−1 (b) ⊆ E ◦ is the set of unknown modules assigned to excitation b
     by σB .

    Consider an excitation b, and its corresponding set σB−1 (b). The restric-
tion of σC to this subset of E ◦ gives the following sub-assignation:

                       σCb : σB−1 (b) → CbP : α → σC (α),                   (8.2)

which is bijective if σB and σC are bijection-pairable, with CbP introduced in
                 Pσ
Definition 7.2. Σ B C , σC−1 (c) and σBc are defined analogously.
    Let σP ref be as defined in Definition 7.5, consider an excitation b, and let

is defined as in (7.7): it equals +1 if the number of such transpositions is

      In this section, we present the main result of the chapter: Theorem 8.7. To
      establish this result, we first introduce two technical lemmas that serve as
      key components in the proof. We first present Lemma 8.4, which addresses
      the signature decomposition of the assignation function.

Lemma 8.4. Let σ ∈ ΣP be a bijective assignation comprised of σB and σC . Its
signature decomposes into

                           sgn(σ ) = sgn(σB ) sgn(σC ).                          (8.3)

Proof. If |Im σB | = 1 (resp. |Im σC | = 1), then sgn(σB ) = 1 (resp.
sgn(σC ) = 1) and (8.3) follows. Otherwise, we have |Im σB | ≥ 2 and
|Im σC | ≥ 2. Assignation σ can be obtained from σP ref by a sequence of
transpositions that swap two unknown modules, denote them α1,1 and
α2,2 , initially assigned to (b1 , c1 ) and (b2 , c2 ) respectively. Since the assig-
nation is bijective, either (i) b1 = b2 , c1 ̸= c2 , (ii) b1 ̸= b2 , c1 = c2 , or (iii)
b1 ̸= b2 , c1 ̸= c2 . Besides, every transposition of case (iii) can be decomposed
into transpositions of type (i) and (ii), as shown in Table 8.1.

                              Conditions with vertex-disjoint walks | 8.3

                                 (i)              (ii)             (i)
          (b1 , c1 )   α1,1    →
                               −           α1,2   −→        α1,2   −
                                                                   →        α2,2
                        ↕                                    ↕
          (b1 , c2 )   α1,2                α1,1             α2,2            α1,2
          (b2 , c1 )   α2,1              α2,1 ↕             α2,1            α2,1
          (b2 , c2 )   α2,2               α2,2              α1,1            α1,1

Table 8.1 Decomposition of a class-(iii) transposition into class-(i) and -(ii)
transpositions. The boxes and arrows indicate the swap operated to obtain
the next column.

    Hence, any assignation σ can be obtained from σP ref by a sequence of
class-(i) and -(ii) transpositions only. Take one such sequence, and denote
its number of transpositions η (σ ). Within this sequence, consider the trans-
positions of class (i) with same fixed excitation b ∈ B P . Combining these
transpositions gives the sub-assignation σCb , defined in equation (8.2). The
number of such transpositions, denoted η (σCb ), gives the parity of σCb . The
same holds for all b ∈ B P , and for the class-(ii) transpositions, for each
c ∈ C P , η (σBc ) is defined analogously. Enumerating all η (σ) transpositions
of the considered sequence allows to rewrite it as a sum:

                       η (σ) =     ∑ η (σBc ) + ∑ η (σCb ).
                                 c∈C P            b∈B P


Since sgn(σ) = (−1)η (σ) by definition, this yields

                       sgn(σ ) = ∏ (−1)η (σBc )           ∏ (−1)η(σCb ) .
                                       c∈C P             b∈B P

    Theorem 8.7 is the central result of this chapter. It extends Proposition 8.1
by adding conditions in terms of vertex-disjoint walks, thereby offering
a stronger statement. The proof builds directly on Lemma 8.5, and essen-
tially consists of constructing a bijective collection of walks ω using the
σB (respectively, σC ) obtained from that lemma. This theorem provides a
necessary condition for the generic local identifiability of networks, and a
sufficient one.

Theorem 8.7. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). If the network is gener-
ically locally identifiable, then there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of
cardinality m◦ with at least one bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP such that one
of the two following conditions holds:

 (A) Either: for each excitation b ∈ B P , the C-parts2 of the walks starting at b
     are vertex-disjoint.
 (B) Or: for each measurement c ∈ C P , the B-parts of the walks ending at c are
     vertex-disjoint.

If there is only one such collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP , then this statement is also
sufficient for the generic local identifiability of network A◦ .

Proof. This proof is derived for an ω satisfying condition (A), from a σB ob-
tained from Lemma 8.5. An analogous argument can be made for condition
(B), from a σC obtained from Lemma 8.5.
   Applying Lemma 8.5 to the considered EM network gives a set of EM
pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ such that there is at least one σB such that
the conditions (a), (b) and (d) of Lemma 8.5 hold. The idea of this proof is to
  2 C-parts and B-parts are defined in Definition 7.6.



                                                                           | 102
                                                      Disproven conjecture | 8.4

      construct a collection of paths ω from this σB , and show that it is bijective, it
      belongs to ΩP and it satisfies condition (A) of Theorem 8.7.
          Condition (a) ensures that σB is connected. Thus, from Definition 8.2, for
      each unknown module αi ∈ E ◦ , there is a walk from its assigned excitation
      σB (αi ) to its start node s(αi ). Besides, condition (d) ensures that for each
      excitation b ∈ B P , there are nP  C b vertex-disjoint walks starting at the end
      nodes of the unknown modules assigned to b by σB , and ending at the mea-
      surements CbP . We construct a collection of walks ω from the connectedness
      of σB and its vertex-disjoint walks: for each unknown module αi ∈ E ◦ , the
      first part of the walk ωi starts at σB (αi ) and follows the sequence assured
      by connectedness. Then the walk goes through αi , follows one of the vertex-
      disjoint walks, and ends at a node of CσP (α ) . Collecting the ωi constructed
                                                   B i
      in this manner for each αi ∈ E ◦ yields the collection of walks ω.
          By construction, for each unknown module αi ∈ E ◦ , the couple made by
      the start node and end node of ωi is an element of P , i.e. (ω B (αi ), ωC (αi )) ∈
      P . Besides, if there are i, j such that ωC (αi ) = ωC (α j ), then by construction
      ω B (αi ) must be different to ω B (α j ). Hence each element of P is not assigned
      more than once by the assignation σ corresponding to ω, i.e. σ is injective.
      Also |E ◦ | = |P |, thus σ is bijective, and so is ω. Finally, by construction,
      condition (A) follows directly from condition (d).
          If there is only one such ω, then there is only one σB satisfying (a),
      (b) and (d), and Lemma 8.5 yields the generic local identifiability of the
      network.



8.4     Disproven conjecture

      Theorem 8.7 generalizes Theorem 3.6, originally established in the full excita-
      tion setting [HGB18], to scenarios with partial excitation and measurement.
      Theorem 8.7 introduces two mutually exclusive conditions, (A) and (B),
      separated by an exclusive or. This naturally raises the question of whether
      a stronger result could be obtained by requiring both conditions to hold
      simultaneously. This motivates the following conjecture, which is refuted
      by the counterexample presented in Example 8.9 and illustrated in Fig. 8.2.

      Conjecture 8.8. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). If the network is
      generically locally identifiable, then there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of


                                                                                | 103
                                               Disproven conjecture | 8.4

cardinality m◦ with at least one bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP such that
both following conditions hold simultaneously:
 (A) For each excitation b ∈ B P , the C-parts of the walks starting at b are
     vertex-disjoint.
 (B) For each measurement c ∈ C P , the B-parts of the walks ending at c are
     vertex-disjoint.
Example 8.9. Consider the EM network depicted in Fig 8.2 (a). Running
Algorithm 2 on it outputs that the whole network is generically locally
identifiable. In this example, since n B nC = m◦ , there is only one option for a
subset P of B × C : the whole set itself: P = B × C = {1, 2} × {3, 4}. There
is only one monomial µ corresponding to bijective collections of walks:
it takes each known module exactly once. There are only three bijective
collections of walks corresponding to that monomial µ, denoted ω b , ω c and
ω d , depicted in Fig 8.2 (b), (c) and (d) respectively. However, none of them
satisfies both (A) and (B) simultaneously:
   • ω1b and ω2b , both starting from the same excitation, meet at node 4,
     which is highlighted with a red star to emphasize this phenomenon.
     Such node will be denoted a Meeting Node (MN), a notion introduced
     in Chapter 9.

   • ω c satisfies neither (A), nor (B). ω1c and ω2c , both starting from the
     same excitation, meet at node 4. ω2c and ω3c , both ending at the same
     measurement, meet at node 3. In Chapter 9, a walk such as ω2c is
     referred to as MN-MN, indicating that it passes through meeting
     nodes in both its B-part and C-part. The MN-MN nature of ω c is what
     makes this EM network identifiable, a phenomenon we examine in
     more detail in Chapter 9.

   • ω2d and ω3d , both ending at the same measurement, meet at node 3.
    The disproven conjecture and its counterexample reveal the deeper
complexity of our problem. This motivates the introduction of a new, more
detailed and powerful framework, one capable of capturing the full range
of subtleties that influence network identifiability in partial excitation and
measurement. Unlike simpler approaches such as Conjecture 8.8, which
overlook crucial hidden mechanisms, this new tool is designed to leave no
detail unaccounted for. While it may appear cumbersome at first glance, its
necessity becomes clear in light of the limitations of previous attempts. The
development and exploration of this tool form the core of Chapter 9.

                                                                    | 105
                The Meta-Walk Graph
                                                                    9
W
            E possess a combinatorial characterization, derived in Chapter 7.
            However, its combinatorial nature makes it difficult to distill prac-
            tical or easily verifiable conditions from it. Walk-based conditions
have been extracted from it in Chapter 8, resulting in Theorem 8.7, which
presents vertex-disjoint walk conditions that extend prior results obtained
in the full excitation or measurement setting [HGB18].
    Still, the situation remains unsatisfactory. The condition in Theorem 8.7
is expressed as a logical OR: it requires either a condition on the excitation
side or a condition on the measurement side. What we seek is a unified
condition : one that simultaneously captures both sides and encodes the
full combinatorial richness of the identifiability problem. This goal led us to
formulate Conjecture 8.8. However, a counterexample refuted it, exposing
a deeper complexity in the problem of network identifiability under partial
excitation and measurement — one that is absent in the full excitation or
measurement cases. The failure of the conjecture spurs the need for a more
sophisticated framework, capable of capturing and exposing the subtleties
that were hidden in Conjecture 8.8.
    Previous results, both from the literature and earlier chapters of this
thesis, highlight the central role of walks — particularly vertex-disjoint
walks — in network identifiability. A key concept introduced in this chapter
is that of Meeting Nodes (MN): nodes at which two non-vertex-disjoint walks
intersect. These nodes are critical, as they represent points where signals

                                                                         | 106
                                         Meeting Nodes and MN-swaps | 9.1

      from different walks interfere, leading to potential information loss. To
      analyze such interactions, we define the notion of an MN-swap: an operation
      that exchanges the suffixes (or prefixes) of walks meeting at a given MN,
      thereby producing a new collection of walks.
         The core idea of this chapter is to construct a new graph, called the
      Meta-Walk Graph, where each vertex corresponds to a bijective collection of
      walks, and vertices are connected by an edge if they can be obtained from
      each other by an MN-swap. The Meta-Walk Graph proves to be a powerful
      tool for analyzing network identifiability. In certain cases, its structure fully
      characterizes whether the network is identifiable or not. We show that
      the Meta-Walk Graph is bipartite, and even multipartite. Moreover, we
      derive quantitative properties, such as the degree of its vertices and its total
      number of vertices, under some assumptions.
          By combining this new framework with the combinatorial characteri-
      zation from Chapter 7, and leveraging tools from graph theory, we obtain
      new sufficient conditions for identifiability.



9.1     Meeting Nodes and MN-swaps

      Meeting nodes generalize the notion of vertex-disjoint walks by providing
      a more precise way to characterize where and how walks fail to be vertex-
      disjoint.

      Definition 9.1. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
      P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP ,
      composed of m◦ walks ω1 . . . ωm◦ . Let ν ∈ W be a node of the network, and
      let λ be an excited or measured node selected by P , i.e. λ ∈ B P ∪ C P .
          We say that νλ is a Meeting Node (MN) of ω when :

        (a) if λ is an excited node : the C-parts of several walks ωi . . . ω j , starting
            from the same excitation λ, meet at ν,
        (b) if λ is a measured node : the B-parts of several walks ωi . . . ω j , ending
            at the same measurement λ, meet at ν.

      The number of such different walks ωi . . . ω j meeting at νλ is the multiplicity
      of the meeting node, denoted πνλ . We denote by Nω the set of all distinct
      meeting nodes of ω. Some special cases require additional clarification:

                                                                                  | 107
                                 Meeting Nodes and MN-swaps | 9.1

   • A single node ν may serve as a meeting node for multiple different
     excitations or measurements λ1 , . . . , λk , this gives rise to multiple
     distinct meeting nodes νλ1 , . . . , νλk .
   • If λ is both excited and measured, it is treated as two distinct nodes:
     the excitation λ B , and the measurement λC . If (a) holds for λ B and
     (b) holds for λC , then we have to distinct meeting nodes, denoted νλB
     and νλC .
   • When the C-part (resp. B-part) of a walk traverses κ times one of
     its meeting nodes νλ — i.e. it contains κ − 1 distinct cycles passing
     through node ν — then this gives rise to κ distinct meeting nodes, one
     for each passage. If ℓ walks make multiple passages, this gives rise to
     κ1 κ2 · · · κℓ distinct meeting nodes, where κl is the number of passages
     of the l-th walk.

Note 9.2. We emphasize that the notion of meeting node is defined relative
to a specific collection of walks ω, and does not depend solely on the
network topology. In the same graph, a node may be a meeting node for
one collection of walks ω b , yet not for another collection ω d . This context-
dependent nature is illustrated in Figure 9.1.
Example 9.3. Consider the EM network depicted in Fig. 9.1, with P =
{(1, 6), (2, 5), (2, 6)}. Let monomial µ ∈ MP be as represented in Fig. 9.1(a).
The set of bijective collections of walks corresponding to µ is composed of
three elements: Ωµ = {ω b , ω c , ω d }, represented in subfigures (b), (c), (d)
respectively. Meeting nodes are highlighted by red stars in the figure. Each
meeting node has multiplicity 2 in this example. We have:

         Nω b = {36 },         Nω c = {36 , 42 },        Nω d = {42 }.

    We introduce the operation of MN-swap, which consists in swapping
the suffixes (or prefixes) of two walks that intersect at a given meeting node.
This operation serves as the adjacency relation in the construction of the
Meta-Walk Graph, as will be detailed in the following sections.
Definition 9.4. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP ,
composed of m◦ walks ω1 . . . ωm◦ . Let νλ be a meeting node (MN) of ω,
where walks ωi and ω j meet.
   An MN-swap is an operation applied to ω to produce a new collection
of walks ω̃, identical to ω except for a modification at the meeting node νλ

                                                                         | 108
                                  Meeting Nodes and MN-swaps | 9.1

                   1                                       5

                        (a) Monomial µ in yellow
                   1                                       5
                                         α1

                                        α2

                                        α3
                   2                                       6

                              (b) ω b : Nω b = {36 }
                   1                                       5

                          3

                   2                                       6

                         (c) ω c : Nω c = {36 , 42 }
                   1                                       5

                                                       4

                         3

                   2                                       6

                              (d) ω d : Nω d = {42 }

Fig. 9.1 The EM network of Example 9.3: dashed modules are unknown,
purple dotted arrows are excitations and blue dotted arrows are measure-
ments. Subfigure (a) represents monomial µ, and subfigures (b), (c), (d)
represent the three bijective ω corresponding to µ. Bold arrows highlight
the changes between ω b and ω c , ω d . Red stars are meeting nodes.

                                                                 | 109
                                   Meeting Nodes and MN-swaps | 9.1

on the two walks ωi and ω j . The modification depends on whether λ is an
excitation or a measurement:

   • If λ is an excitation, the suffixes of walks ωi and ω j after the meeting
     node ν are exchanged. That is, ω̃i follows the initial segment of ωi up
     to ν, then continues along the suffix of ω j ; similarly for ω̃ j .

   • If λ is a measurement, the prefixes of walks ωi and ω j before the
     meeting node ν are exchanged. That is, ω̃i follows the prefix of ω j up
     to ν, then continues along the suffix of ωi ; and analogously for ω̃ j .

The collections of walks ω and ω̃ correspond to the same monomial µ and
are said neighbors. An MN-swap is a bidirectional operation: if ω̃ can be
obtained from ω by an MN-swap at meeting node νλ , then ω can likewise
be obtained from ω̃ by an MN-swap at the same node νλ .

Note 9.5. Recall the type (i) and type (ii) transpositions introduced in the
proof of Lemma 8.4. An MN-swap applied to a bijective collection of walks
corresponds to performing one of these transpositions on its associated
assignation, thereby flipping its signature. This observation will be formal-
ized in Proposition 9.9.
Example 9.3 (continued). Consider the EM network depicted in Fig. 9.1. ω b
and ω c are neighbors: they can be obtained from each other by an MN-
swap at meeting node 36 . Similarly, ω c and ω d are neighbors: they can be
obtained from each other by an MN-swap at meeting node 42 .
   Proposition 9.6 establishes that the operation of an MN-swap is well-
defined: it preserves the bijective character of the collection of walks.

Proposition 9.6. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP ,
composed of m◦ walks ω1 . . . ωm◦ . Let νλ be a meeting node (MN) of ω, where
walks ωi and ω j meet. The collection of walks ω̃, obtained by an MN-swap between
walks ωi and ω j at νλ , is bijective.

Proof. We assume that λ is an excitation, an analogous reasoning can be
done if it is a measurement. Say that ωi and ω j are the walks corresponding
to unknown edges αi and α j respectively, and they end at measurements
ci and c j respectively. In other words, the assignation corresponding to ω
assigns (λ, ci ) to αi and (λ, c j ) to α j . Since ω is bijective, no other unknown
edge is assigned to neither (λ, ci ) nor (λ, c j ). Now consider ω̃. Since it is
obtained by an MN-swap between walks ωi and ω j at νλ , its corresponding

                                                                            | 110
                                                     The Meta-Walk Graph | 9.2

      assignation assigns (λ, c j ) to αi and (λ, ci ) to α j . The rest of ω̃ is the same as
      ω, hence no other unknown edge is assigned to neither (λ, ci ) nor (λ, c j ),
      and ω̃ is bijective.



9.2     The Meta-Walk Graph

      Armed with the notions introduced in the previous section, we are now
      ready to present the central concept of this chapter. As shown in Chapter 7,
      bijective collection of walks play a key role in the determinant of KP , espe-
      cially their signature. Moreover, performing an MN-swap transforms one
      bijective collection of walks into another, with the important property of
      flipping the signature.
         This observation motivates the construction of a new graph, where each
      vertex represents a bijective collection of walks, and edges correspond to
      MN-swaps between them. We refer to this graph as the Meta-Walk Graph.
      By studying the structure and properties of the Meta-Walk Graph, we aim to
      uncover deeper insights into the identifiability of the underlying network.

      Definition 9.7. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
      P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP . The Meta-Walk
      Graph Gµ of this monomial is defined as follows:

         • The vertices of the graph are the bijective collections of walks corre-
           sponding to µ, i.e. the set of vertices of Gµ is Ωµ .

         • Two collections of walks are adjacent in Gµ (i.e. connected by an edge)
           if they are neighbors in the sense of Definition 9.4, i.e. if they can be
           obtained from each other by an MN-swap.

      The Meta-Walk Graph is undirected since MN-swaps are a bidirectional
      operation, see Definition 9.4.

      Example 9.3 (continued). The Meta-Walk Graph of the monomial depicted in
      Fig. 9.1(a) is given in Fig. 9.2. Observe that the EM network of Example 8.9
      has the exact same Meta-Walk Graph for its only monomial corresponding
      to bijective collections of walks, composed of all known modules.
         We begin by recalling the fundamental definitions of multipartite and
      bipartite graphs.

                                                                                    | 111
                                             The Meta-Walk Graph | 9.3


                          ωb            ωc           ωd

Fig. 9.2 The Meta-Walk Graph of the monomial µ of Example 9.3, depicted
in Fig. 9.1(a). The three bijective collections of walks corresponding to µ are
represented in Figure 9.1 (b), (c), (d).


Definition 9.8. [W+ 01] A multipartite graph is a type of graph in which
the vertices can be divided into distinct independent sets such that no two
vertices within the same set are adjacent. If a graph can be divided into two
sets, it is called a bipartite graph. In general, for k sets, the graph is called a
k-partite graph.

    Building on Note 9.5, Proposition 9.9 establishes a key qualitative prop-
erty of the Meta-Walk Graph: it is bipartite, and even multipartite.

Proposition 9.9. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP . Its Meta-Walk Graph
Gµ is

   • k-partite, where each partition set consists of all the collections of walks
     corresponding to the same bijective assignation,

   • bipartite: the partition set denoted + (resp. −) contains all bijective collec-
     tions of walks whose associated bijective assignations have positive (resp.
     negative) signature.

Proof. If there is no edge in Gµ , then k = |Ωµ | and the result is trivial. Other-
wise, consider one edge {ω, ω̃ } and the corresponding bijective assignations
σ and σ̃ respectively. ω and ω̃ are just one MN-swap away from each other.
For their corresponding assignations, this results in σ and σ̃ being just one
transposition away from each other. Hence σ ̸= σ̃ and sgn(σ ) = − sgn(σ̃ ).
Since the argument holds for every edge of Gµ , the proof follows.

Example 9.3 (continued). Consider again the EM network depicted in Fig. 9.1,
and its Meta-Walk Graph represented in Fig. 9.2. Each bijective collection of
walks of Ωµ = {ω b , ω c , ω d } corresponds to a different bijective assignation:
the Meta-Walk Graph Gµ is 3-partite. Gµ is also bipartite : ω b and ω d have
same signature, opposite to the one of ω c .

9.3     Quantitative properties

      The degree (i.e. number of neighbors) of a bijective collection of walks in
      the Meta-Walk Graph can be easily computed from the multiplicities of its
      meeting nodes.

      P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , a monomial µ ∈ MP and its Meta-Walk Graph
      Gµ . Let ω be a bijective collection of walks of Ωµ , i.e. a vertex of Gµ . The degree
      (i.e. number of neighbors) of ω in Gµ is given by

      νλ , see Definition 9.1.

      can be extracted from πνλ elements. Summing this for all meeting nodes of
      ω yields (9.1).

          A bijective collection of walks is said MN-MN if one of its walks has
      meeting nodes in both its B-part and C-part. This notion is closely related
      to the concept of independent meeting nodes, as defined below.
      Definition 9.11. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
      P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a bijective collection of walks ω ∈ ΩP ,
      composed of m◦ walks ω1 . . . ωm◦ .
         We say that ω is MN-MN if at least one of the walks ωi of ω has a
      meeting node in its B-part and a meeting node in its C-part1 .
         1 The B-part and C-part of a walk ω
                                                    i are introduced in Definition 7.6.

   Besides, consider a collection of walks ω with at least two meeting nodes
 λ
ν1 1 and ν2λ2 . These meeting nodes are said independent if either:

  (i) λ1 and λ2 are both excitations (resp. measurements);
 (ii) λ1 is an excitation (resp. measurement), λ2 is a measurement (resp.
                                                           λ
      excitation), and there is no walk ωi for which both ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are
      meeting nodes.

Those two notions are closely related: a collections of walks is MN-MN if it
has at least two non-independent meeting nodes.

Example 9.3 (continued). In the example of Fig. 9.1, ω c is MN-MN: 36 and
42 are not independent. ω b and ω d are not MN-MN.
  Lemma 9.12 establishes that MN-swaps preserve the independence of
meeting nodes.

Lemma 9.12. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), and a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ . Let ω be a bijective collection of walks of ΩP , with
                                  λ
two independent meeting nodes ν1 1 and ν2λ2 . Consider ω̃, a collection of walks
                                                             λ
obtained by MN-swap between walks ωi and ω j meeting at ν1 1 . In ω̃, ν2λ2 is still a
                                             λ
meeting node, and is still independent of ν1 1 .
                                   λ
Proof. The independence of ν1 1 and ν2λ2 arises from two possible cases:
(i) and (ii) of Definition 9.11. This proof addresses each case separately,
following the same order.

  (i) λ1 and λ2 are both excitations (resp. measurements). Consider they are
      both excitations, an analogous proof can be made if they are both
      measurements. If ν2λ2 is meeting node of neither ωi nor ω j , then the
      result is trivial. Otherwise, there are three cases : ν2λ2 is meeting node
      of : (a) ωi , (b) ω j , (c) ωi and ω j . Consider case (a), and denote ωk one
      of the walks meeting ωi at ν2λ2 . By Definition 9.1, ω j and ωi come from
      the same excitation λ1 , and ωk and ωi come from the same excitation
      λ2 . Hence λ1 = λ2 . Denote ω̃l the walk in ω̃ corresponding to ωl in
      ω, for any l. There are two cases:
             λ
        A. ν1 1 comes before ν2λ2 in ωi . In ω̃, ν2λ2 is now a meeting node for
                                           λ
           ω̃ j and ω̃k . For ω̃ j , both ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are meeting nodes, and they
           are independent.

            and ω̃k . For ω̃i , both ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are meeting nodes, and they are
            independent.

      The argument for case (b) follows symmetrically, with the roles of
      indices i and j reversed. In case (c), the same argument as in (a) (or
      equivalently (b)) applies directly, without any modification.
  (ii) λ1 is an excitation (resp. measurement), λ2 is a measurement (resp. exci-
                                                     λ
       tation), and there is no walk for which both ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are meeting nodes.
       Consequently, neither ωi nor ω j meets any other walk at ν2λ2 . By Defi-
                                                                                     λ
      nition 9.4, performing an MN-swap between walks ωi and ω j at ν1 1
      does not impact other walks than ωi and ω j , i.e. ω̃l = ωl for all l ̸= i, j.

                        λ                                     λ
      for which both ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are meeting nodes in ω̃: ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are
      still independent.

Example 9.13. Consider the EM network depicted in Fig. 9.3, with P =
