   If there is no MN-MN bijective collection of walks in a connected compo-
nent of the Meta-Walk Graph, then its number of vertices can be explicitly
computed, as shown by Proposition 9.14.

Proposition 9.14. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , a monomial µ ∈ MP and its Meta-Walk Graph
Gµ . Let ω be a bijective collection of walks of Ωµ with at least one meeting node,
and such that there is no MN-MN bijective collection of walks in the connected
component of ω in Gµ , denoted Gµ,ω . The vertex set of Gµ,ω is denoted by Ωµ,ω .
                                     λ        λη
Denote the meeting nodes of ω by ν1 1 , . . . , νηωω , where ηω is the number of meeting
nodes of ω. The number of collections of walks in Ωµ,ω is even, and is given by
the multiplicities of the meeting nodes of ω:

                            Ωµ,ω = πν1 λ1 ! · · · πνηω ληω !                       (9.2)

                                                    Quantitative properties | 9.3

Proof. We proceed by construction. We start with Ω̃ = ∅, and fill this
set until Ω̃ = Ωµ,ω . Consider a meeting node ν1 1 of ω, of multiplicity
                                                      λ

πν1 λ1 . There are πν1 λ1 ! permutations of the πν1 λ1 walk prefixes/suffixes
    λ
at ν1 1 (including ω), which all give bijective collections of walks of same
monomial µ. They can all be obtained from ω by a sequence of MN-swaps,
hence belong to Ωµ,ω , the vertex set of the connected component of ω in Gµ .
We append those πν1 λ1 ! collections of walks in Ω̃.
    Now consider another meeting node ν2λ2 of ω. Since there is no MN-
MN collection of walks in Ωµ,ω , ν1 1 and ν2λ2 are independent in the sense
                                       λ

of Definition 9.11. Hence, by Lemma 9.12, for each ω̃ ∈ Ω̃, ν2λ2 is also a
meeting node, still independent of ν1 1 . Thus, for each ω̃ ∈ Ω̃, there are
                                          λ

πν2 λ2 ! permutations of the πν2 λ2 walk prefixes/suffixes at ν2λ2 (including
ω̃), which all give bijective collections of walks of same monomial µ They
can be obtained by a sequence of MN-swaps from ω̃, hence belong to Ωµ,ω
as well. Appending those new collections of walks to Ω̃ for all ω̃ ∈ Ω̃
gives |Ω̃| = πν1 λ1 ! πν2 λ2 !. Repeating this reasoning for all meeting nodes
yields the result. Since all meeting nodes are independent, each collection
of walks of Ωµ,ω is counted once and only once. The multiplicity πν1 λ1 ≥ 2
by definition, hence the result is even.

9.4     Sufficient conditions on identifiability

      Proposition 9.15 (a) is a straightforward extension of Proposition 8.1 (ii), and
      can be seen as an immediate corollary of the combinatorial characterization
      of Theorem 7.9.
          Proposition 9.15 (b) is more refined. It leverages the structure of the
      Meta-Walk Graph to formulate a more specific and insightful condition.
      This result is derived by combining Proposition 9.14, which characterizes
      the size of connected components in the absence of MN-MN configurations,
      with Proposition 9.15 (a).

      Proposition 9.15. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). We have the two
      following sufficient conditions for generic local network identifiability:

        (a) If there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a mono-
            mial µ ∈ MP for which the number of corresponding bijective collections of
            walks Ωµ is odd, then the network is generically locally identifiable.
        (b) If there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a
            monomial µ ∈ MP such that in Ωµ there is an odd number of bijective
            collection of walks with no meeting node, and no MN-MN bijective collection
            of walks, then the network is generically locally identifiable.

      Proof. (a) Assume there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality
      m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP for which the number of corresponding
      bijective collections of walks Ωµ is odd. Then the repetition r (µ) (defined
      in (7.9)) cannot be zero, and the network is generically locally identifiable
      by Theorem 7.9.
          (b) Assume there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality
      m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP such that in Ωµ there is an odd number of
      bijective collection of walks with no meeting node, and no MN-MN bijective
      collection of walks. Since there is no MN-MN bijective collection of walks,
      Proposition 9.14 applies to each connected component of Gµ , except for the
      isolated vertices. It follows that the total number of bijective collections
      of walks — excluding isolated vertices — is even. These isolated vertices
      correspond precisely to the bijective collections of walks with no meeting
      node, and their number determines the parity of r (µ). If the number of such
      walk collections is odd, then Ωµ is odd, and the argument of (a) concludes
      the proof.


                                                                               | 118
                             Sufficient conditions on identifiability | 9.5

Example 9.13 (continued). Applying Proposition 9.15 (a) to the EM network
of Example 9.13 ensures its generic local network identifiability. The same
reasoning applies to Example 8.9.
   The following fundamental result from graph theory is a direct conse-
quence of the handshaking lemma [W+ 01].
Lemma 9.16. Consider an undirected graph. If the number of vertices with an
even degree is odd, then the total number of vertices is odd.
Proof. We know from the handshaking lemma that for any undirected graph,
the sum of the degrees of all vertices equals twice the number of edges
[W+ 01]. Hence the number of vertices with an odd degree is even, which
yields the result.

    By combining Theorem 7.9 with Lemma 9.16, we obtain our most general
sufficient condition, stated in Theorem 9.18. While this condition may
initially seem difficult to apply, it is important to note that the degree of
a bijective collection of walks in Gµ can be explicitly computed using the
expression derived in Proposition 9.10. Before presenting Theorem 9.18, we
introduce the following notation to clarify its statement.
Definition 9.17. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), a set of EM pairs
P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , a monomial µ ∈ MP and its Meta-Walk
Graph Gµ . We denote the set of bijective collections of walks with an even
degree in Gµ as follows:

                 even deg
               Ωµ           ≜ {ω ∈ Ωµ | 0 ≡ deg(ω ) mod 2},                 (9.3)

where deg(ω ) is the degree of ω (i.e. neighbors number) as vertex of Gµ .

Theorem 9.18. Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ). If there exists a set of
EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP such that
  even deg
 Ωµ        is odd, then the network is generically locally identifiable.
Proof. Assume there exists a set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality
                                                  even deg
m◦ , and a monomial µ ∈ MP such that Ωµ                    is odd. Applying
Lemma 9.16 on the Meta-Walk Graph Gµ yields that |Ωµ | is odd. Then,
Proposition 9.15 (a) directly implies the result and completes the proof.

Example 9.13 (continued). Applying Theorem 9.18 to the EM network of
Example 9.13 ensures its generic local network identifiability. The same
reasoning applies to Example 8.9.

9.5     Characterization of identifiability for a specific case

      Consider an EM network ( A◦ , A• , B, C ), and assume we only have access
      to one set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of cardinality m◦ . If the two following
      conditions are satisfied:
        (a) There is only one monomial µ corresponding to bijective collections of
            walks, i.e. |MP | = 1;
       (b) The Meta-Walk Graph Gµ of this monomial is connected;
      then the Meta-Walk Graph Gµ entirely characterizes the generic local iden-
      tifiability of network A◦ . In other words, all the information needed to
      decide whether the network is generically locally identifiable is encoded in
      the structure of Gµ .
          The procedure to verify identifiability under these conditions proceeds
      as follows. Two cases must be considered:
        (i) If Ωµ , the number of vertices of Gµ , is odd, then Proposition 9.15 (a)
            implies the generic local identifiability of A◦ .
       (ii) If Ωµ is even, we leverage the bipartite structure of Gµ . Specifically,
            we count how many ω belong to the + and − partition sets. If these
            counts differ, then the repetition satisfies r (µ) ̸= 0, and Theorem 7.9
            guarantees that A◦ is generically locally identifiable. Conversely, if the
            counts are equal, then r (µ) = 0, and the network A◦ is not generically
            locally identifiable.

          We provide a brief explanation regarding the need for the two conditions
      (a) and (b), as well as the assumption on P :
        (a) If |MP | > 1, then it suffices to find a single monomial µ ∈ MP for
            which the network is generically locally identifiable to conclude that
            A◦ is generically locally identifiable. However, to conclude that A◦
            is not generically locally identifiable, all monomials in MP must be
            examined and found to yield zero repetition.
       (b) If Gµ is not connected, we lack information about the relative signa-
           tures of bijective collections of walks belonging to different connected
           components. That is, Gµ does not encode whether two such ω have
           the same or opposite signatures. As a result, the approach described
           in (ii) cannot be applied.

                                                                              | 120
                                                                Conclusion | 9.6

        (c) If we have access to more than one set of EM pairs P ⊆ B × C of
            cardinality m◦ , the same problem as in (a) arises.



9.6     Conclusion

      This chapter introduced a novel approach to studying network identifi-
      ability. By leveraging the notions of meeting nodes and MN-swaps, the
      Meta-Walk Graph reveals the structural properties of the network that di-
      rectly influence identifiability. When combined with the combinatorial
      characterization developed in Chapter 7, this framework yields new suffi-
      cient conditions on identifiability.
          Constructing the Meta-Walk Graph of an EM network allows one to
      distill and visualize the key features that govern identifiability. In the
      specific setting of Section 9.5, we demonstrated how the Meta-Walk Graph
      fully encapsulates all the relevant information for identifiability, and enables
      a straightforward identifiability check.
         However, in the general case — where multiple choices for P and µ exist
      — several Meta-Walk Graphs need to be considered, rendering exhaustive
      analysis impractical. Moreover, when the Meta-Walk Graph is not con-
      nected, essential information about the relative signatures of walks across
      connected components is missing, making identifiability undeterminable
      from the sole knowledge of the Meta-Walk Graph.
          These limitations underscore the intrinsic complexity of the identifiabil-
      ity problem. Even with the Meta-Walk Graph, it remains a fundamentally
      hard and deeply combinatorial challenge in the general case.




                                                                              | 121
     PART III
Signal selection for
   identifiability
       Optimal signal selection is
                                                        10
                         NP-hard


P
      ART III is dedicated to the development of algorithms for selecting
      nodes to excite and measure in order to guarantee the generic local
      identifiability of the network. Since performing excitations and mea-
surements is often costly in practice, our objective is to achieve identifiability
while minimizing the number of such interventions.
    In this chapter, we establish that the problem of finding such optimal se-
lection — i.e., excitation and measurement sets of minimal total cardinality
that ensure identifiability — is NP-hard. NP-hard problems are at least as
difficult as the hardest problems in NP, the class of problems whose solu-
tions can be verified in polynomial time. Informally, NP-hard problems are
widely regarded as computationally intractable for large instances, meaning
that no efficient (i.e. polynomial-time) algorithm is known for solving them
in general. As a result, one typically relies on heuristic, approximate, or
problem-specific methods for practical purposes.
     We begin by reviewing foundational notions in computational complex-
ity, including the classes P, NP, and the notion of NP-hardness. We then
introduce the classical NP-complete problem 3SAT, which plays a central
role in complexity theory and serves as a basis for our NP-hardness proof.
Finally, we show that our signal selection problem is NP-hard by construct-
ing a polynomial-time reduction from 3SAT. That is, we demonstrate how

                                                                          | 123
                                           Classes P, NP, and NP-hard | 10.1

       any instance of 3SAT can be transformed into an instance of our problem.
       Consequently, if our problem could be solved in polynomial time, then 3SAT
       could also be solved in polynomial time, which would imply P = NP, a
       conclusion that is widely believed to be false.



10.1     Classes P, NP, and NP-hard

       Understanding the computational difficulty of a problem is essential, partic-
       ularly when the design or analysis task involves combinatorial structures
       or large-scale systems. In this section, we provide a brief overview of the
       fundamental complexity classes P, NP, and the concept of NP-hardness.
       Additional background can be found in the references [Sip96, Pap03, AB09].


10.1.1 The class P
       The class P (for Polynomial time) consists of all decision problems that can be
       solved by a deterministic Turing machine in time that is upper bounded by
       a polynomial function of the size of the input. That is, a problem is in P if
       there exists an algorithm whose worst-case running time is O(nk ) for some
       constant k, where n is the size of the input.
           Problems in P are typically regarded as tractable, meaning they can be
       efficiently solved even for large instances, in contrast to problems outside
       of this class. Examples of problems in P include shortest path computa-
       tion, maximum flow, matrix multiplication, and solving systems of linear
       equations.


10.1.2 The class NP
       The class NP (for Nondeterministic Polynomial time) contains all decision
       problems for which a proposed solution can be verified in polynomial time
       by a deterministic Turing machine. In other words, even if we do not know
       how to efficiently find a solution, we can efficiently check whether a given
       solution is correct. For instance, while factoring a large integer into primes
       is not known to be solvable in polynomial time, checking whether a given
       factorization is correct is straightforward and fast.



                                                                              | 124
                                         Classes P, NP, and NP-hard | 10.1




      Fig. 10.1   Euler diagram for classes P, NP, NP-complete and NP-hard
      [Wik25a].


          The acronym NP reflects the historical perspective of nondeterministic
      computation. A nondeterministic Turing machine is a hypothetical model
      of computation that can “guess” a solution nondeterministically and then
      verify it in polynomial time. Though such machines do not exist physically,
      this abstraction gives rise to the class NP. Importantly, P ⊆ NP, since any
      problem whose solution can be computed in polynomial time can also have
      its solution verified in polynomial time.
         A classic example of a problem in NP is the Boolean satisfiability problem
      (SAT), where the goal is to determine whether there exists a truth assignment
      that satisfies a given Boolean formula.


10.1.3 NP-complete and NP-hard problems
      A decision problem is said to be NP-complete if it is both in NP and as hard
      as any other problem in NP, in the sense that every problem in NP can be
      reduced to it in polynomial time. The notion of polynomial-time reduction
      allows to formally compare the difficulty of computational problems.
         The class of NP-complete problems represents the most challenging
      problems in NP: if any one of them can be solved in polynomial time, then


                                                                           | 125
                       A fundamental NP-complete problem: 3SAT | 10.2

       every problem in NP can also be solved in polynomial time, implying P =
       NP, a famous open question in theoretical computer science.
           A problem is called NP-hard if it is at least as hard as the hardest prob-
       lems in NP, meaning that every problem in NP can be reduced to it in
       polynomial time. Unlike NP-complete problems, an NP-hard problem is
       not required to be in NP, and may not even be a decision problem. For
       instance, optimization problems like the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP)
       in its optimization form are NP-hard but not in NP.


10.1.4 Significance in practice
       In control theory and system identification, many design or verification
       problems turn out to be NP-hard. This means that, unless P = NP, there
       does not exist a polynomial-time algorithm that solves all instances of the
       problem efficiently. Consequently, for large-scale instances, one must resort
       to heuristic, approximate, or problem-specific methods.
          In the next section, we will present one of the most fundamental NP-
       complete problems, known as 3SAT, which will serve as a basis for proving
       the NP-hardness of our problem.



10.2     A fundamental NP-complete problem: 3SAT

       The Boolean satisfiability problem (SAT) is a central problem in computer
       science and mathematical logic. Given a Boolean formula, the goal is to
       determine whether there exists a truth assignment to its variables that makes
       the entire formula evaluate to true. The SAT problem was the first problem
       shown to be NP-complete, as demonstrated by Stephen Cook in his seminal
       work on the complexity of theorem-proving procedures [Coo23].
           A particularly important restriction of SAT is the 3SAT problem, where
       each clause in the Boolean formula contains exactly three literals. Despite
       this apparent simplification, 3SAT remains NP-complete, and it is frequently
       used as a base problem for NP-hardness reductions due to its simple and
       constrained structure. Formally, an instance of 3SAT is defined as follows:
          • Let { x1 , x2 , . . . , xn } be a set of Boolean variables, where each vari-
            able xi ∈ {0, 1} for i = 1, 2, . . . , n, with 1 representing true and 0
            representing false.

                                                                                | 126
                                                         NP-hardness proof | 10.3

          • Let Φ be a Boolean formula expressed in 3-conjunctive normal form
            (3CNF), meaning Φ = c1 ∧ c2 ∧ · · · ∧ cm , where ∧ stands for logical
            AND and each clause c j is a disjunction (logical OR) of exactly three
            literals.

          • A literal is either a variable xi or its negation ¬ xi .

          • The question is: Does there exist a truth assignment (i.e., a mapping
            of each variable to true or false) that makes Φ evaluate to true?

       As an example, consider the following Boolean formula in 3SAT form:

       Φ = ( x1 ∨ x2 ∨ x3 ) ∧ ( x1 ∨ x2 ∨ ¬ x3 ) ∧ (¬ x1 ∨ x2 ∨ x3 ) ∧ (¬ x1 ∨ ¬ x2 ∨ x3 )∧
                          (¬ x1 ∨ x2 ∨ ¬ x3 ) ∧ ( x1 ∨ ¬ x2 ∨ ¬ x3 ) ∧ (¬ x1 ∨ ¬ x2 ∨ ¬ x3 ).

       This formula has seven clauses, each containing exactly three literals. To
       satisfy Φ, one must assign ( x1 , x2 , x3 ) to (false, true, false) so that every
       clause has at least one literal that evaluates to true. This example illustrates
       that, while verifying a solution to a 3SAT instance is straightforward, finding
       such a solution can be computationally challenging.
           The 3SAT problem is NP-complete, and this result is foundational in
       computational complexity theory. It provides a starting point for proving
       that other problems are NP-hard via polynomial-time reductions. Due
       to its generality and simplicity, it is a canonical problem in the study of
       computational intractability.
          We refer the reader to the standard texts such as [GJ02, Pap03] for a more
       detailed presentation of SAT, 3SAT, and their central role in the theory of
       NP-completeness.



10.3     NP-hardness proof

       Now that we have introduced the complexity classes P, NP, and NP-hard,
       and presented the 3SAT problem, we proceed to establish the NP-hardness
       of our problem by constructing a reduction from 3SAT.




                                                                                    | 127
                                                  NP-hardness proof | 10.3

Theorem 10.1. Consider a network with unknown modules and known modules.
The following problems are NP-hard.

 (A) Let no node be a priori excited, and all nodes be a priori measured. The
     problem of selecting a minimal excitation set that ensures generic global
     identifiability of all unknown modules.
 (B) Let all nodes be a priori excited, and no node be a priori measured. The
     problem of selecting a minimal measurement set that ensures generic global
     identifiability of all unknown modules.
 (C) Let no node be a priori excited, and some nodes be a priori measured. The
     problem of selecting a minimal excitation set that ensures generic global
     identifiability of all unknown modules.
 (D) Let some nodes be a priori excited, and no node be a priori measured. The
     problem of selecting a minimal measurement set that ensures generic global
     identifiability of all unknown modules.
 (E) Let some nodes be a priori excited, and some nodes be a priori measured.
     The problem of selecting an excitation set and a measurement set of minimal
     total cardinality, that ensures generic global identifiability of all unknown
     modules.
  (F) Let no node be a priori excited, and no node be a priori measured. The
      problem of selecting an excitation set and a measurement set of minimal
      total cardinality, that ensures generic global identifiability of all unknown
      modules.

Proof. We first prove that (A) is NP-hard using a reduction from 3SAT. Given
an arbitrary 3CNF formula Φ, we construct a network G3SAT (Φ) as follows.

Claim 10.2. Consider a 3CNF formula Φ, its corresponding network G3SAT (Φ)
with all nodes a priori measured, and i ∈ {1, . . . , β}. The unknown module αix is
generically globally identifiable if and only if at least one node among {wi , wis , wi }

Proof of Claim 10.2. Applying Theorem 3 of [SCVdH22] yields that αix is
generically globally identifiable if and only if there is a walk starting at an
excitation and ending at wis . Given the topology of G3SAT (Φ), this excitation
can only be among the three nodes {wi , wis , wi }.

Claim 10.3. Consider a 3CNF formula Φ with β variables, and its corresponding

are generically globally identifiable with exactly β excitations, then for each i ∈

Proof of Claim 10.3. From Claim 10.2, for each i ∈ {1, . . . , β}, there must be
at least one excitation among {wi , wis , wi } for the generic global identifi-
ability of αix . Since there are exactly β excitations, it must be exactly one
excitation among those three nodes.

   Transforming any 3CNF formula Φ into the graph G3SAT (Φ) takes poly-
nomial time, even using a brute-force construction. Thus, if we could solve
(A) in polynomial time, then we could also solve 3SAT in polynomial time,
by following the procedure below, where each step takes polynomial time:
   1. Construct network G3SAT (Φ) from the given formula Φ,
   2. Find a minimal excitation set B ⋆ such that all unknown modules are
      generically globally identifiable,
   3. Construct τ ⋆ from B ⋆ .
But that would imply P = NP, which is widely believed to be false. We
conclude that (A) is NP-hard.
    We now show that (C) is NP-hard. If it could be solved in polynomial
time, then so could (A), since the latter is a special case of the former where
all nodes are assumed to be measured. But we have already established that
(A) is NP-hard. It follows that (C) must also be NP-hard.
    We now show that (E) is NP-hard using a similar argument. If this
problem could be solved in polynomial time, then so could (A), since the
latter is a special case of (E) in which no node is a priori excited, and all
nodes are already measured, hence no additional measurements need to be
selected. As we have already shown that (A) is NP-hard, it follows that (E)
is NP-hard as well.
   The NP-hardness of (B) and (D) can be established through an analogous
argument, using a dual construction of the network.
    Finally, the NP-hardness of (F) can be established using the same reduc-
tion as for (A). We construct the same graph G3SAT (Φ) and invoke Lemma 1
from [SCVdH23], which requires measuring the nodes wit and wcj      t for all

i ∈ 1, . . . , β and j ∈ 1, . . . , γ.


                                                                                        | 132
                      Algorithms for signal
                                                      11
                                  selection


W
         E have established that the problem of optimal signal selection is
         computationally intractable in the general case, as no polynomial-
         time algorithm exists, unless P = NP. Consequently, attempting
to compute exact optimal solutions becomes impractical for large-scale
networks due to the exponential growth in computational complexity.
    Given the intractability of the problem, we relinquish the pursuit of guar-
anteed optimality. With the exception of the Exhaustive Search — which
is primarily employed to benchmark the optimality gap of our proposed
methods — none of the four other algorithms presented can ensure optimal
solutions. Instead, our focus shifts to designing algorithms that aim to
find high-quality solutions within reasonable computational time, thereby
maintaining tractability even as network size increases.
    To address this challenge, our algorithms build upon a combination
of algebraic conditions, walk-based criteria, and algorithmic frameworks
developed earlier in this thesis. By leveraging these diverse tools, we pro-
pose five distinct algorithms, each embodying a different methodological
approach. To evaluate their performance, we constructed a dataset compris-
ing networks of varying topologies and sizes. The chapter concludes with
an analysis and discussion of the algorithms’ performance on this dataset.
Implementations available in our GitHub repository [LH25].

                                                   Tools for algorithm design | 11.1


11.1     Tools for algorithm design

       This section compiles the key tools that underpin the design of our algo-
       rithms. Some of these tools are novel contributions — such as the lower
       bound on the number of required signals and the monotonicity property of
       identifiability — while others are concise, black-box recaps of results intro-
       duced earlier, including Algorithms 2 and 1. Finally, we revisit the results
       from Chapter 8 through the lens of algorithm design. We examine how each
       condition could be leveraged in practice, and this analysis naturally leads
       to heuristics that will guide the development of the method presented in
       Section 11.2.5.


11.1.1 Lower bound
       In this section, we derive a lower bound on the number of signals required
       to ensure the identifiability of all unknown modules. This bound will serve
       as guidance for selecting an initial iterate or as a stopping criterion for the
       algorithms developed in this chapter.
            To guarantee identifiability, the number of excitation–measurement pairs
       must be greater or equal to the number of unknowns, i.e. n B · nC ≥ m◦ .
       To determine the lower bound, we minimize the total number of signals,
       i.e. the sum of excitations and measurements. Denoting x ≜ n B ∈ N and
       y ≜ nC ∈ N, the problem reduces to:

                                      min     f ( x, y) = x + y                   (P1)
                                       x,y

                                       s.t.   x · y ≥ m◦
                                              x, y ∈ N

       A lower bound of our signal selection problem is given by the optimal
       condition of (P1), which is given by the proposition below.

       Proposition 11.1. The optimal solution of (P1) is

11.1.2 Generic local identifiability test
       In Chapter 5, we developed Algorithm 2, a procedure that exploits the
       algebraic condition and the pseudo-generic nature of local identifiability, as
       established in Theorem 5.12. The algorithm efficiently tests these conditions
       on a randomly chosen matrix A consistent with the network structure, and
       returns the correct result with probability 1.
       Algorithm 2 :

      input The network graph Gnet = (W , {E • , E ◦ }), excitation set B and mea-
            surement set C

     output The generic local identifiability of each distinct unknown module

          Algorithm 2 serves as a central and efficient tool in the development of
       our signal selection algorithms. It enables the verification of generic local
       identifiability for any given EM network, i.e. for any network topology, and

                                                                                   | 138
                                            Tools for algorithm design | 11.1

       any excitations and measurements sets. As will be seen in the following
       sections, our signal selection procedures rely heavily on this algorithm.
            Algorithm 2 is based on the matrix K = B⊤ T ⊤ ⊗ CT I ◦ , as defined
                                                                   

       in Definition 5.8, where T = ( I − A)−1 . This matrix has dimensions
       n B nC × m◦ , and on large-scale instances, repeatedly computing K can be-
       come computationally expensive.
           Consider a procedure that repeatedly invokes Algorithm 2 to search for
       minimal excitation and measurement sets that ensure generic local identifi-
       ability of a given network. Although the excitation and measurement sets
       vary across calls, the underlying network topology remains fixed through-
       out the procedure.
           This observation enables a significant computational optimization: in-
       stead of recomputing the matrix K = B⊤ T ⊤ ⊗ CT I ◦ at each iteration, one
                                                            

       can precompute and store the full matrix Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ just once at
                                                                      

       the beginning. Then, for each specific excitation and measurement config-
       uration, the relevant submatrix of K can be efficiently extracted from Kfull ,
       via careful indexing to respect the Kronecker product structure of Kfull .
           However, implementing Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ naively involves first con-
                                                       

       structing the full L2 × L2 Kronecker product matrix T ⊤ ⊗ T, and then se-
       lecting a subset of its columns via multiplication with I ◦ . This approach is
       computationally inefficient and memory-intensive, especially for large net-
       works, as the vast majority of the entries in T ⊤ ⊗ T are ultimately discarded
       by the multiplication with I ◦ .
           To address this, we implemented a dedicated function, kron_submatrix,
       which computes Kfull in a more efficient manner. Instead of forming the
       full Kronecker product, it directly computes only the relevant entries se-
       lected by the post-product I ◦ , thereby reducing both computation time and
       memory usage. The implementation is available at [LH25]: identifiable
       / algebraic_identifiability_test / kron_submatrix.


11.1.3 Monotonicity of identifiability
       Identifiability — whether generic or not, global or local — exhibits a form of
       monotonicity with respect to excitations and measurements, in the following
       sense:

          • Adding excitations or measurements to an identifiable network pre-
            serves identifiability.

                                                                             | 139
                                            Tools for algorithm design | 11.1

         • Conversely, removing excitations or measurement signals from a non-
           identifiable network cannot render it identifiable.

         In our setting, we are interested in minimal configurations that ensure
      identifiability : cases where the network is identifiable, but the removal of
      any single excitation or measurement breaks this property.
          This type of monotonicity is reminiscent of similar phenomena in other
      domains. For example, in graph connectivity, once a graph is connected,
      adding edges cannot make it disconnected; and conversely, a disconnected
      graph cannot be made connected by removing edges. The minimal number
      of edges that ensures connectivity corresponds to a spanning tree, just as
      we seek minimal sets of signals ensuring identifiability.


11.1.4 SIMUG and pseudotree covering algorithms
      In section 3.2, we presented Algorithm 1 from [DSC+ 22] : a procedure to
      compute a set of excitations that guarantees generic global identifiability
      of the input network, when all nodes are measured, some modules are
      unknown and some modules are known. It first constructs an edge-disjoint
      SIMUG covering, then assigns one excitation per SIMUG root, and finally
      prunes unnecessary excitations by testing whether global identifiability
      is preserved upon their removal. Note that there is no guarantee on the
      optimality of the number of excitations obtained by Algorithm 1.
      Algorithm 1 :

     input The network graph Gnet = (W , {E • , E ◦ })

    output A set of excitations B such that the network is generically globally
           identifiable under full measurement

         As discussed in Section 3.2.1, [CSVdH21] develops a closely related
      algorithm for the case without known modules, where SIMUGs are replaced
      by pseudotrees.


11.1.5 Heuristics from walk-based conditions
      In this section, we revisit the conditions established in Chapter 8 and explore
      how they can be leveraged in the design of our algorithms. For the reader’s
      convenience, we provide a brief summarized statement of each condition

                                                                             | 140
                                      Tools for algorithm design | 11.1

below, using “identifiability” as a shorthand for “generic local network
identifiability”.

   • Proposition 8.1 (i), summarized as :
                  Identifiability ⇒ ∃ P : ∃ bijective ω ∈ ΩP
     This necessary condition is too permissive: it is satisfied by a wide
     range of network configurations that are not generically locally identi-
     fiable, as evidenced by empirical analysis.

   • Proposition 8.1 (ii), summarized as :
                ∃ P : ∃ only 1 bijective ω ∈ ΩP ⇒ Identifiability
     This sufficient condition is overly restrictive, particularly for highly
     connected networks. Empirical observations show that most generi-
     cally locally identifiable networks fail to meet this criterion. Moreover,
     verifying the uniqueness of a bijective collection of walks requires
     checking all possible combinations — a task that quickly becomes
     computationally intractable when designing algorithms intended to
     scale with arbitrary network sizes and topologies.

   • Theorem 8.7 — the necessary condition, summarized as :
     Identifiability ⇒ ∃ P : ∃ bijective ω ∈ ΩP without MN in its C-parts
                                                          (resp. B-parts)
     This necessary condition refines Proposition 8.1 (i) by being more
     restrictive, while being practical to implement:

        – To enforce the bijectivity constraint, an assigation σ can be con-
          structed incrementally while iterating over B and C , ensuring
          that it remains both bijective and connected at each step. Such a
          bijective and connected assignation σ guarantees the existence of
          a corresponding bijective collection of walks ω.
        – The condition requiring the absence of meeting nodes is equiv-
          alent to the existence of vertex-disjoint walks, which can be ef-
          ficiently verified by checking the rank of the corresponding T
          matrices, as established in Proposition 3.3.

   • Theorem 8.7 — the sufficient condition, summarized as :
     ∃ P : ∃ only 1 bijective ω ∈ ΩP without MN in its C-parts ⇒ Identif.
                                                     (resp. B-parts)
     Similar to Proposition 8.1 (ii), this sufficient condition is difficult to
     handle in practice: verifying the uniqueness of such a collection of


                                                                       | 141
                                                  Algorithms description | 11.2

             walks ω would require enumerating all possible candidates, which
             becomes computationally intractable for large and highly connected
             networks.

          As discussed in Section 8.4, it is tempting to strengthen the necessary
       condition Theorem 8.7 by enforcing the vertex-disjoint walk constraints
       (A) and (B) on both the excitation and measurement sides. This leads to
       Conjecture 8.8, a necessary condition which was disproven in Section 8.4
       through a counterexample.
           In our approach, we use this (disproven) necessary condition as a fil-
       tering step for candidate EMP sets. Validated candidates are then tested
       using Algorithm 2 to check whether they ensure generic local network
       identifiability.
          Relying on this disproven condition as a necessary test will inevitably
       exclude some valid solutions and result in optimality loss. Nevertheless, we
       will show that, when combined with other algorithmic tools, its practical
       impact can be mitigated.



11.2     Algorithms description

       This section presents our signal selection algorithms, built upon the tools
       introduced in Section 11.1. All implementations are available in the GitHub
       repository [LH25].


11.2.1 Exhaustive Search
       Algorithm 3 presents our Exhaustive Search: starting from the lower bound
       calculated in Proposition 11.1, the procedure tests the generic local identifia-
       bility of all possible combinations of excitations and measurements, using
       the algebraic criterion of Corollary 5.14. As soon as the criterion is met, the
       algorithm returns the considered excitation and measurement sets, with the
       guarantee that their total cardinality is optimal.
           The notation Kfull,I∗ denotes the submatrix of Kfull consisting of the rows
       indexed by I and all columns. It is important to highlight that the set Ξ
       constructed at line 6 is combinatorially large. Moreover, the lower bound
       provided in Proposition 11.1 is only achieved in ideal scenarios, where


                                                                               | 142
                                                     Algorithms description | 11.2

      Algorithm 3 Exhaustive Search
      Require: The network graph Gnet = (W , {E ◦ , E • })
      Result: Excitation set B ⋆ , measurement set C ⋆ such that the EM network
          (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable, and |B ⋆ | + |C ⋆ | minimal
       1: Randomly sample matrices A◦ , A• consistent with E ◦ , E •
       2: A : = A◦ + A• and T : = ( I − A )−1
       3: Compute Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ with function kron_submatrix
                                             
                   √            l   ◦
                                          m
       4: nR : =        m◦ + √m ◦
                                    ⌈ m ⌉
       5: for n = nR → 2L do
       6:     Construct Ξ := {(B , C) : B ⊆ W , C ⊆ W , |B| + |C| = n}, set of all
          combinations of excitations and measurements with total cardinality n
       7:     for (B , C) ∈ Ξ do
       8:          Compute I , the row indices corresponding to (B , C) in Kfull
       9:          if rank Kfull,I∗ = m◦ then
      10:                return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) := (B , C)


      each excitation-measurement pair uniquely identifies a distinct unknown
      module — a situation that is rarely encountered in practice.
          As a result, the Exhaustive Search is expected to be highly inefficient, as it
      must evaluate a vast number of combinations before finding a valid solution.
      In practice, the method becomes intractable for networks of moderate size.
      For instance, our experiments show that even on networks with as few as
      20 nodes, the algorithm becomes prohibitively slow.
         This outcome is expected, given the inherently naive and exhaustive
      nature of the algorithm. Nevertheless, despite its simplicity, it is the only
      method among those we propose that guarantees optimality. As such, its
      solutions serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating the performance of
      our other, more scalable algorithms on small-scale networks.


11.2.2 Pruning Search
      Algorithm 4 outlines our Pruning Search strategy. The procedure begins
      with all nodes excited and all nodes measured, ensuring that the initial
      configuration guarantees identifiability. It then performs npass independent
      pruning passes, where each pass consists of arbitrarily removing one excita-


                                                                                     | 143
                                              Algorithms description | 11.2

tion or measurement and subsequently verifying whether identifiability is
preserved. The algorithm finally returns the best solution across these npass
trials, defined as the configuration with the lowest total number of signals.

Algorithm 4 Pruning Search
Require: The network graph Gnet = (W , {E ◦ , E • }), parameter npass
Result: Excitation set B ⋆ , measurement set C ⋆ such that the EM network
    (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable
 1: Randomly sample matrices A◦ , A• consistent with E ◦ , E •
 2: A : = A◦ + A• and T : = ( I − A )−1
 3: Compute Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ with function kron_submatrix
                                     

 4: Initialize B0 : = W and C0 : = W
 5: return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) : = pruning_multipass(Gnet , Kfull , B0 , C0 , npass )


    Each pruning pass follows the same principle: starting from the fully
excited and measured configuration, it iteratively attempts to remove ex-
citations and measurements while preserving generic local identifiability.
The order in which signals are considered for removal is randomized in
each pass, which impacts the quality of the resulting solution. Repeating
this randomized pruning procedure npass times increases the likelihood of
finding a solution with minimal cardinality.
   To improve readability and facilitate reuse across algorithms, we define a
companion function, pruning_multipass, which manages the execution of
the npass pruning passes. Notation B0 ⊎ C0 at line 6 of Algorithm 5 denotes
the multiset union, where common elements of B0 and C0 appear twice.
Line 16 returns the current solution if it is as good as the lower bound nR
derived in Section 11.1.1. Line 18 updates the best solution found so far
with the current one, if its number of signals is lower.
    Compared to the Exhaustive Search, the Pruning Search leverages ran-
domization to significantly improve computational efficiency. While this
comes at the cost of losing the guarantee of optimality, it yields a tractable
algorithm. However, the Pruning Search remains agnostic to the structural
properties of the problem — in particular, it does not exploit the network
topology or the walk-based conditions developed earlier. The subsequent
algorithms address this limitation by incorporating structural insights and
tailored heuristics into their design, aiming to enhance both efficiency and
solution quality.

                                                                                 | 144
                                                  Algorithms description | 11.2




Algorithm 5 pruning_multipass
Require: Gnet , Kfull , B0 , C0 , npass
Result: B ⋆ , C ⋆ such that (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable
            √  l m◦ m
 1: nR : =      m◦ + √ ◦
                          ⌈ m ⌉
 2: n0 : = |B0 | + |C0 |
 3: Initialize B ⋆ : = B0 , C ⋆ : = C0 and n⋆ : = n0
 4: if n0 > nR then
 5:     for p = 1 → npass do
 6:          for λ randomly picked in B0 ⊎ C0 do
 7:              if λ was picked from B0 then
 8:                 Bnew := B \ λ and Cnew := C
 9:              else
10:                 Bnew := B and Cnew := C \ λ
11:              Compute I , the indices of (Bnew , Cnew ) in Kfull
12:              if rank Kfull,I∗ = m◦ then
13:                  B := Bnew and C := Cnew
14:          n := |B| + |C|
15:          if n ≤ nR then
16:              return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) := (B , C)
17:          else if n < n⋆ then
18:              B ⋆ := B , C ⋆ := C and n⋆ := n




                                                                               | 145
                                                  Algorithms description | 11.2

11.2.3 Pseudotree Search
      Algorithm 6 presents our Pseudotree Search strategy, which closely follows
      the general procedure of the Pruning Search. The key difference lies in the
      initialization step. Instead of starting from full excitation and full measure-
      ment, the algorithm begins by invoking the pseudotree allocation algorithm
      from [CSVdH21]. As discussed in Section 3.2.1, this algorithm provides an
      excitation set that guarantees generic global identifiability when all modules
      are unknown and all nodes are measured.
          In our context, since some modules are known, this initialization likely
      leads to an overestimated excitation set, though this is not formally guaran-
      teed. To obtain an initial measurement set, we apply the same pseudotree
      allocation procedure to the transposed network, in the sense of [CSLVdH24]:
      the adjacency matrix is transposed, and the roles of excitations and mea-
      surements are swapped. The transposed network of a given network Gnet is
                    ⊤ .
      denoted by Gnet

      Algorithm 6 Pseudotree Search
      Require: The network graph Gnet = (W , {E ◦ , E • }), parameter npass
      Result: Excitation set B ⋆ , measurement set C ⋆ such that the EM network
          (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable
       1: Randomly sample matrices A◦ , A• consistent with E ◦ , E •
       2: A : = A◦ + A• and T : = ( I − A )−1
       3: Compute Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ with function kron_submatrix
                                           

       4: B0 : = solution of the pseudotree algorithm of [CSVdH21] with input
          Gnet where all modules are assumed unknown, and all nodes measured
       5: C0 : = solution of the pseudotree algorithm of [CSVdH21] with input
            ⊤ where all modules are assumed unknown, and all nodes measured
          Gnet
       6: return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) = adding_pruning_multipass(Gnet , Kfull , B0 , C0 , npass )



         Although empirical evidence suggests that the resulting excitation and
      measurement sets often ensure generic local identifiability, no guarantee is
      provided. Therefore, we begin with an addition phase, where excitations
      and measurements are incrementally added at random until the network
      becomes generically locally identifiable. This is followed by a pruning
      phase, analogous to Algorithm 5. The entire procedure is repeated npass


                                                                                 | 146
                                                  Algorithms description | 11.2

times, and the best (i.e., lowest cardinality) solution among these runs is
returned, as described by Algorithm 7.

Algorithm 7 adding_pruning_multipass
Require: Gnet , Kfull , B0 , C0 , npass
Result: B ⋆ , C ⋆ such that (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable
            √  l m◦ m
 1: nR : =      m◦ + √ ◦
                          ⌈ m ⌉
 2: n0 : = |B0 | + |C0 |
 3: Initialize B ⋆ : = B0 , C ⋆ : = C0 and n⋆ : = n0
 4: if n0 > nR then
 5:     for p = 1 → npass do
 6:          Compute I , the indices of (B0 , C0 ) in Kfull
 7:          while rank Kfull,I∗ < m◦ do
 8:              Randomly add an excitation or measurement to (B0 , C0 )
 9:              Update I accordingly
10:          end while
11:          for λ randomly picked in B0 ⊎ C0 do
12:              if λ was picked from B0 then
13:                 Bnew := B \ λ and Cnew := C
14:              else
15:                 Bnew := B and Cnew := C \ λ
16:              Compute I , the indices of (Bnew , Cnew ) in Kfull
17:              if rank Kfull,I∗ = m◦ then
18:                  B := Bnew and C := Cnew
19:          n := |B| + |C|
20:          if n ≤ nR then
21:              return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) := (B , C)
22:          else if n < n⋆ then
23:              B ⋆ := B , C ⋆ := C and n⋆ := n

11.2.4 SIMUG Search
      Algorithm 8 presents our SIMUG Search strategy, which differs from the
      Pseudotree Search only in its initialization step. Instead of using the pseu-
      dotree allocation algorithm from [CSVdH21], it initializes with the SIMUG
      allocation algorithm introduced in [DSC+ 22], discussed in Section 11.1.4,
      detailed in Section 3.2, and denoted as Algorithm 1.

      Algorithm 8 SIMUG Search
      Require: The network graph Gnet = (W , {E ◦ , E • }), parameter npass
      Result: Excitation set B ⋆ , measurement set C ⋆ such that the EM network
          (Gnet , B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) is generically locally identifiable
       1: Randomly sample matrices A◦ , A• consistent with E ◦ , E •
       2: A : = A◦ + A• and T : = ( I − A )−1
       3: Compute Kfull = T ⊤ ⊗ T I ◦ with function kron_submatrix
                                           

       4: B0 : = Algorithm 1 (Gnet )
       5: C0 : = Algorithm 1 (Gnet    ⊤ )

       6: return (B ⋆ , C ⋆ ) = adding_pruning_multipass(Gnet , Kfull , B0 , C0 , npass )


          Empirical evidence also indicates that, unlike in Algorithm 6, the initial-
      ized excitation and measurement sets frequently fail to guarantee identifia-
      bility. As a result, the additional augmentation step (adding pass) is often
      required in this case to achieve identifiability. The lack of identifiability of
      the initialized excitation and measurement sets can be explained as follows.
      The initial excitation set, B0 , is generated by Algorithm 1, which, under the
      assumption that all nodes are measured, produces a set of excitations that
      guarantees generic global identifiability. Since the algorithm is designed
      to minimize the size of B0 (although without guaranteeing optimality),
      the resulting set tends to be relatively restrictive and small. The initial
      measurement set, C0 , is obtained in an analogous manner by applying Al-
      gorithm 1 to the transposed network. Consequently, both B0 and C0 are
      typically restrictive and small, and the corresponding EM network obtained
      by combining them is often non-identifiable, hence the frequent use of the
      adding pass. This issue was less pronounced for Algorithm 6, since known
      modules were treated as unknown during the initialization. As a result, the
      initial sets B0 and C0 were less restrictive and often ensured identifiability.



                                                                                 | 148
                                                 Algorithms description | 11.2

11.2.5 Merging Search
      Algorithm 9 presents our Merging Search strategy, which leverages the
      heuristics introduced in Section 11.1.5. The algorithm initializes the exci-
      tation and measurement sets as the start and end nodes of the unknown
      modules respectively, an initialization that guarantees identifiability. It then
      iteratively alternates between B-merges (excitation merges) and C-merges
      (measurement merges), aiming to reduce the total number of signals while
      preserving identifiability.
          In a B-merge, the algorithm considers replacing a group of excitations
      by one of their common ancestors — i.e., a node w from which there exists
      a path to each excitation in the group. Guided by the heuristics from
      Section 11.1.5, it checks that such a merge does not introduce any meeting
      node in either the B-parts or the C-parts. This verification is based on the
      current assignations σB and σC , which are updated throughout the merging
      process. Additionally, it ensures that the pair (σB , σC ) remains bijective.
      If these walk-based conditions are satisfied, the algorithm then tests for
      generic local identifiability using the algebraic criterion, and performs the
      merge if the condition holds. C-merges are carried out analogously, on the
      measurement nodes.
          After the merging phase, the algorithm performs npass pruning passes to
      eliminate potentially redundant signals and reduce the overall cardinality of
      the solution. This entire procedure is repeated nrun times, and the solution
      with the lowest cardinality among all runs is returned.
          There are two variants of the merging subroutines: the ranking version
      and the uniform version. The distinction between the two lies in the order
      in which candidate pairs (w, candidates) are selected and evaluated for
      merging. The uniform version samples these pairs uniformly at random.
      In contrast, the ranking version leverages the network structure to guide
      the search more efficiently. For instance, it is pointless to consider potential
      B-merges toward a sink node. More promising candidates are nodes from
      which paths reach many excitation points, an intuition captured by the
      ranking vector ranking, which determines the processing order in the
      ranking version.
         Empirically, with a single run, the ranking version tends to outperform
      the uniform version, both in speed and in producing solutions with fewer
      signals. However, when multiple runs are allowed, the uniform version


                                                                              | 149
                                           Algorithms description | 11.2

explores a broader space of solutions and can eventually yield better results,
whereas the deterministic ranking version does not benefit from repeated
runs.
    The number of potential merges grows combinatorially with the network
size, making exhaustive exploration increasingly impractical. To mitigate
this, a stopping criterion is introduced to terminate the search early, with its
threshold adapted to the size of the network.
   To keep the document concise, we include only the pseudocode for the
merging_ranking_B subroutine, presented in Algorithm 10. Its measure-
ment version merging_ranking_C is analogous, while merging_uniform_B
and merging_uniform_C are simpler variants using random ordering. All
implementations are available in the GitHub repository [LH25].




                                                                        | 150
                                               Algorithms description | 11.2

11.3     Dataset description

       To evaluate and compare the performance of our algorithms, we generated
       a dataset comprising networks of varying sizes and topologies, available in
       the GitHub repository [LH25]. This section provides a detailed description
       of the dataset.


11.3.1 Network topologies
       We consider five distinct graph types, each exhibiting different structural
       properties, presented in Fig. 11.3. This selection is intended to cover a
       broad spectrum of network topologies, thereby aiming for generality in our
       evaluation. The chosen graph types include both synthetic structures and
       topologies inspired by practical applications or real-world networks.
           Erdős–Rényi (ER) networks are a class of random graphs introduced
       in the late 1950s by Paul Erdős and Alfréd Rényi. In the ER model, a
       graph is constructed by connecting each pair of nodes independently with
       fixed probability. This simple generative rule leads to graphs with homoge-
       neous degree distributions and no inherent community structure. Despite
       their simplicity, ER networks serve as a fundamental baseline in network
       science and are widely used for benchmarking algorithms due to their
       well-understood theoretical properties.
           Lattice graphs are regular graphs in which each node is connected to its
       nearest neighbors in a structured, grid-like pattern. In two dimensions, they
       are often represented as nodes placed on the points of a square grid, where
       each node is connected to its four immediate neighbors. These graphs are
       highly regular and exhibit strong locality, making them useful for modeling
       spatial systems or physical networks such as electrical grids, crystal lattices,
       or image processing tasks. Their regularity and simplicity make them
       a common benchmark for evaluating the behavior of algorithms under
       structured topologies.
           The IEEE standard power networks are a collection of benchmark test cases
       widely used in the power systems community for the evaluation of algo-
       rithms in areas such as state estimation, optimization, and control. These
       networks, maintained and distributed by the IEEE, provide realistic topolo-
       gies, line parameters, and operational data that reflect practical constraints


                                                                               | 153
                                                        Dataset description | 11.3

and characteristics of real-world power grids. They serve as a standard ref-
erence to ensure reproducibility and comparability of results across studies,
and are available at [Ins25].
   Random geometric graphs (RGGs) are constructed by placing L nodes
uniformly at random in a metric space (typically the unit square in R2 ),
and connecting pairs of nodes whose Euclidean distance is below a given
threshold. These graphs capture spatial locality and are widely used to
model wireless communication networks, sensor networks, and other sys-
tems where connections depend on physical proximity. Unlike Erdős–Rényi
graphs, RGGs naturally incorporate geometric constraints and clustering,
making them useful for studying network algorithms in spatial settings.
    Watts–Strogatz graphs are generated through a rewiring process that
interpolates between regular lattices and random graphs, aiming to repli-
cate the small-world phenomenon observed in many real-world networks.
The construction starts from a ring lattice where each node is connected to
its k nearest neighbors. Then, with a rewiring probability p, each edge is
randomly rewired to a new node. This model yields graphs that simultane-
ously exhibit high clustering (like regular lattices) and short average path
lengths (like random graphs), thereby capturing the small-world property.
These graphs are often used to model social, biological, and technological
networks where local cohesion and global connectivity coexist.




    IEEE Standard                      Erdős-Rényi                            Lattice




               Watts-Strogatz (Small-World)          Random Geometric Graph



Fig. 11.3 The five network topologies of our dataset. Blue edges represent
known modules, red edges unknown ones.


                                                                                        | 154
                                                                 Results | 11.4

11.3.2 Network metrics
       For each of those five network topologies, we generated networks with
       number of nodes L ∈ {10, 15, 20, 30, 40, 50, 100} and with:

          • Average degree 2m/L ∈ [2, 4], where m is the number of edges (both
            unknown and known)

          • Ratio of unknown modules m◦ /m = 1/3

       For each network instance, we sampled 10 different configurations of un-
       known modules that adhere to these criteria.



11.4     Results

       We applied the five algorithms described in Section 11.2 to the network
       dataset introduced in Section 11.3. All algorithms were run with npass = 10,
       and the Merging Search was run with nrun = 10. Each network instance
       includes 10 different configurations of unknown modules. For each configu-
       ration, we executed every algorithm 10 times (except for Exhaustive Search,
       which is deterministic). We then computed the average number of selected
       signals and the average computation time across these 100 executions. The
       results are summarized in Table 11.1 and Table 11.2, respectively. The Ex-
       haustive Search was executed only on networks with 16 nodes or fewer, as it
       becomes computationally prohibitive for larger instances. Implementation
       available in our GitHub repository [LH25].


11.4.1 Discussion
       An initial observation from Table 11.1 is that, although the Pruning, Pseu-
       dotree, SIMUG, and Merging strategies do not offer formal guarantees
       of optimality, their empirical performance on small networks (where the
       optimal solution is available via Exhaustive Search) remains consistently
       close to optimal. The most significant deviation is observed for Pseudotree
       Search on the ER15 instance, which yields a score of 6.22, corresponding to
       an optimality gap of 3.67%. While these results do not provide theoretical
       assurances regarding performance on larger networks, they offer encour-
       aging empirical evidence of the algorithms’ practical effectiveness in such
       settings.

                                                                           | 155
                                                            Results | 11.4

    To facilitate comparison across algorithms, for each network in the
dataset, we highlight in yellow the lowest score, referred to as a “win” for
the corresponding algorithm. The final row of the table summarizes the
total number of such wins achieved by each method. From this overview, it
is evident that Pseudotree Search and SIMUG Search frequently yield the
best results. Nevertheless, both Pruning Search and Merging Search also
secure a non-negligible number of wins, indicating their competitiveness
in certain scenarios. Importantly, no clear pattern emerges from the table
that would suggest a single algorithm consistently outperforms the others
across specific network topologies or sizes.
    It can be observed that the Pseudotree Search generally outperforms the
SIMUG Search. At first glance, this result may seem counterintuitive, as the
Pseudotree Search treats known modules as unknown, thereby using less in-
formation and offering a less precise initialization. The explanation is likely
as follows: as discussed earlier, the initialization phase of the SIMUG Search
often fails to guarantee identifiability, necessitating an additional augmenta-
tion step (the adding pass). By contrast, in the Pseudotree Search, the adding
pass is almost never required, as supported by empirical evidence. The
adding pass in SIMUG Search operates by randomly introducing additional
excitations or measurements until identifiability is achieved. This additional
layer of randomness allows the SIMUG Search to explore a broader range
of possible solutions, whereas the Pseudotree Search, with its deterministic
initialization, does not benefit from this extra exploratory step.
    An examination of Table 11.2 reveals that, with the exception of Ex-
haustive Search, all proposed algorithms complete execution in under one
second for networks containing up to 50 nodes. For networks with 100
nodes, execution times remain efficient, with all algorithms completing in
less than 13 seconds.
   In conclusion, all four algorithms demonstrate practical relevance. For
an real-world application, a reasonable strategy would be to execute all
four methods and select the best-performing solution among them. This
approach leverages the complementary strengths of the algorithms and
maximizes the likelihood of obtaining a high-quality result.
