                                                                 | 158
 PART IV
Conclusion
                                                               12
                                                         Conclusion


       T
                O CONCLUDE this thesis, we first summarize the main research con-
                tributions developed throughout the chapters. We then reflect on the
                relationship between generic local and generic global identifiability.
       While the former is known to be a necessary condition for the latter, whether
       it is also sufficient remains an open question. To date, no counterexample
       has been found.
            Finally, we outline several perspectives for future research, including po-
       tential extensions of the theoretical framework, algorithmic improvements,
       and applications to different problem settings.



12.1     Research outcomes

       In Chapter 4, we introduced the concept of local identifiability and es-
       tablished its connection to the local injectivity of a carefully constructed
       function h. A key result of this chapter is that, generically, analyzing net-
       works with scalar modules is equivalent to working with analytic transfer
       functions. This equivalence justifies restricting the analysis to the scalar case
       throughout the remainder of the thesis, with the assurance that the results
       extend to the broader class of analytic systems. This simplification not only
       streamlines the mathematical treatment but also highlights the underlying
       combinatorial and structural nature of the identifiability problem.


                                                                                | 160
                                               Research outcomes | 12.1

    Chapter 5 developed an algebraic characterization of local scalar-
coordinate injectivity using tools from manifold theory. As part of this
analysis, we established that scalar-coordinate injectivity is a pseudo-generic
property. By applying this characterization to the function h, we obtained
an algebraic criterion for local identifiability and demonstrated that it too is
pseudo-generic. This led to necessary and sufficient conditions for generic
local identifiability (i) for a single unknown module, (ii) for a subset of
unknown modules, and (iii) for all unknown modules in the network.
Crucially, the pseudo-generic nature of these conditions allows them to be
verified numerically with probability one by evaluating them on a randomly
sampled instance of the network. Based on this principle, we developed an
efficient algorithm that determines the generic local identifiability of any
given network.
    However, this algebraic condition offers limited insight into the struc-
tural or dynamical behavior of the network. Chapter 7 reformulated the
rank condition established in Chapter 5 into a combinatorial framework at
the network level. This translation reveals how the identifiability of network
modules is fundamentally governed by the topology of the network and
the structure of walk-based patterns within it.
    Building on the algebraic and combinatorial characterizations devel-
oped in Chapters 5 and 7, Chapter 8 derives walk-based conditions for
identifiability. Specifically, it introduces a necessary condition based on the
existence of vertex-disjoint walks, along with a sufficient condition of simi-
lar nature. These results generalize earlier findings from the full excitation
(or full measurement) setting [HGB18], extending them to the more general
case of partial excitation and measurement.
    Chapter 9 introduced a novel perspective through the construction of the
Meta-Walk Graph, a new graph derived from the original network in which
each node represents a collection of walks. This framework provides deeper
insight into the combinatorial complexity underlying the identifiability
problem. By analyzing the structure and connectivity of the Meta-Walk
Graph, the chapter established new sufficient conditions for identifiability
and offered a powerful tool for understanding the interplay between walk-
based structures and network identifiability.
     In Part III, we began by establishing the NP-hardness of the problem
of selecting optimal sets of excitations and measurements that ensure iden-
tifiability. Given this computational intractability, our focus shifted to the

                                                                        | 161
       Gap between generic global and generic local identifiability | 12.2

       design of efficient algorithms capable of producing high-quality solutions
       within reasonable computational time, even for large-scale networks.
           To tackle this challenge, we developed five algorithms that build upon
       the algebraic conditions, walk-based criteria, and structural insights in-
       troduced in the earlier parts of the thesis. These algorithms are designed
       to exploit the combinatorial structure of the network while maintaining
       computational tractability. We conducted a comparative evaluation of the
       algorithms’ performance on a dataset of networks with diverse topologies
       and sizes.



12.2     Gap between generic global and generic local identifiability

       Generic local identifiability is a necessary condition for generic global iden-
       tifiability, as it captures the same concept but restricted to a local neighbor-
       hood of the network. While it is a priori a weaker notion, the extent of this
       weakness is not immediately clear. This section investigates the relationship
       between the two concepts and explores whether generic local identifiability
       may, in practice, be nearly as strong as generic global identifiability.
          In cases of full excitation or full measurement, the notions of generic
       local and generic global identifiability are equivalent, as established by
       Proposition 5.13 in Section 5.2. Similarly, for separable EM networks, the
       two notions coincide, as demonstrated in Proposition 6.5.
           In the general case of partial excitation, partial measurement, and arbi-
       trary network topology, the relationship between generic global and generic
       local identifiability remains an open question. A natural intuition might
       suggest that if a function is locally injective almost everywhere, it should
       also be globally injective almost everywhere. However, this reasoning is
       flawed, as illustrated by the classical counterexample f ( x ) = x2 , which is
       locally injective on R0 , but not globally injective on R.
           If a counterexample to the equivalence between generic local and global
       identifiability exists, we believe that it is likely to be found in a small
       network. This view is based on intuition rather than on any formal result.
       To investigate this, and with the assistance of Anne Rubbens, we manually
       examined all structurally interesting networks consisting of 3 nodes, 4
       unknown edges, 2 excitations, and 2 measurements. Despite this exhaustive
       inspection, no counterexample was found.

                                                                               | 162
Gap between generic global and generic local identifiability | 12.2

    To extend this investigation, we developed a script aimed at finding a
network that is generically locally identifiable but not generically globally
identifiable. Such a network would serve as a counterexample, demonstrat-
ing that local identifiability is not sufficient to guarantee global identifiability.
The script is available at [LH25] : identifiable / gap_global_local.
    The script focuses exclusively on the identifiability of the entire network,
assessing generic local identifiability via the rank condition on the matrix K.
Several classes of networks are excluded from the search space to streamline
the analysis:

   • Networks with full excitation or full measurement are omitted, as the
     equivalence between local and global identifiability in these cases has
     already been established.

   • Disconnected graphs are excluded, since their identifiability proper-
     ties can be inferred from those of their connected components.

   • Graphs with self-loops are excluded from our analysis, as we observed
     that they tend to produce misleading results : what appear to be
     counterexamples but are not. This behavior is likely due to numerical
     instabilities or pathological cases in the underlying system equations,
     which cause MATLAB’s solve function to perform unreliably on such
     structures.

   • Known edges are not included, because we believe that if counterex-
     amples exist, then there is one counterexample without known edges.
     This view is based on intuition rather than on any formal result.

   • We restrict our analysis to EM networks satisfying m◦ = n B nC . This
     choice is motivated by the following considerations:

         – If m◦ > n B · nC , the system is underdetermined, with more
           unknowns than equations, and thus not generically identifiable.
         – If m◦ < n B · nC , the system contains some redundant or non-
           informative equations. In such cases, the EM network can often
           be reduced to an equivalent one satisfying m◦ = n B nC (a hy-
           pothesis we consider plausible). Moreover, we expect that any
           potential counterexample to the sufficiency of generic local iden-
           tifiability is most likely to arise in this critical case where the
           number of unknowns matches the number of equations.


                                                                            | 163
                                                               Pespectives | 12.3

       Excluding the special cases discussed above, we have exhaustively exam-
       ined all EM networks with up to five unknown edges and no known edges.
       Across this exhaustive search — covering more than two million distinct
       EM networks — no counterexample to the sufficiency of generic local iden-
       tifiability for generic global identifiability was found. Script available at our
       GitHub repository [LH25].



12.3     Pespectives

12.3.1 Improvements on algorithms for signal selection
       Our signal selection algorithms rely on Corollary 5.14, but do not currently
       exploit the more detailed structural insights provided by Theorem 5.12. In
       particular, there is potential to enhance these algorithms by incorporating
       information about which specific modules are not identifiable.
           When testing whether a given set of excitations and measurements
       ensures generic local identifiability — via the rank condition on the matrix
       K — a failure to satisfy this condition indicates that the system is not fully
       identifiable. In such cases, the structure of ker K through its orthogonality
       relations, can reveal which modules are unidentifiable and which may still
       be identifiable. This finer-grained information could be leveraged to guide
       the search process more intelligently, potentially improving the efficiency
       of our algorithms.
           Besides, there remains potential to extract additional identifiability con-
       ditions from the Meta-Walk Graph approach. While a few useful properties
       have already been derived, it is possible that not all relevant insights have
       been uncovered. In particular, the degree of a bijective collection of walks
       in Gµ , which can be explicitly computed using the expression in Propo-
       sition 9.10, may offer further opportunities. We believe that combining
       this expression with Theorem 9.18 could lead to actionable identifiability
       conditions, especially in specific network topologies or structured cases.
           Currently, our algorithms do not directly leverage the conditions de-
       rived from the Meta-Walk Graph framework. However, this presents an
       opportunity: it may be possible to design a dedicated algorithm that ex-
       plicitly incorporates the structural properties and identifiability conditions
       revealed by the Meta-Walk Graph. Such an approach could enhance both


                                                                                | 164
                                                              Pespectives | 12.3

      the theoretical understanding and practical performance of signal selection
      strategies.


12.3.2 Identifiability of a subset of unknown modules
      Part I gives an algebraic characterization for the identifiability of any set of
      unknown modules: a single unknown module, a subset of them or all of
      them. However, Parts II and III focus exclusively on the identifiability of all
      unknown modules simultaneously — a property commonly referred to as
      network identifiability. This distinction highlights promising perspectives for
      further work.
          Indeed, when the conditions presented in Part II indicate that the net-
      work is not identifiable, they do not provide any information about the
      identifiability of individual modules. In particular, they do not reveal
      whether some unknown modules may still be identifiable, nor do they
      specify which ones.
          If one seeks to extend the conditions presented in Part II to address the
      identifiability of only a subset of the unknown modules, composed of more
      than one element, the situation becomes more complex. In particular, one
      cannot simply treat the remaining, non-targeted unknown modules as if
      they were known.
          One challenge is that the identifiability of a subset of unknown modules
      is not a truly generic property, but rather a pseudo-generic one. Unlike
      generic properties, pseudo-genericity lacks the convenient implication that
      validity at a single point ensures validity almost everywhere.
          The identifiability condition is formulated in terms of the orthogonality
      of ker K, as stated in Theorem 5.12. This orthogonality can be reformulated
      as the linear independence condition in (5.4), which in turn can be expressed
      as (5.5). For the case of full network identifiability, combining these condi-
      tions across all unknown modules leads to a single rank condition. This
      can be further reduced to the non-vanishing of a determinant, ultimately
      yielding a combinatorial characterization.
          However, in the general case — when only a subset of modules is
      considered — we do not yet know how to simplify the condition into a
      more tractable form. As it stands, the condition is difficult to apply in
      practice. Developing a more manageable formulation remains an open
      problem for future research.

          Part III presents algorithms for designing excitation and measurement

                                                                              | 165
                                                             Pespectives | 12.3

       sets that guarantee generic local identifiability of the entire network. How-
       ever, when the objective shifts to identifying only a subset of the unknown
       modules — potentially with fewer signals than those required for full net-
       work identifiability — the current algorithms do not directly address this
       more targeted scenario.
           Nevertheless, the Pruning Search procedure can be readily adapted to
       this setting, leveraging the algebraic characterization provided by Theo-
       rem 5.12, which explicitly determines which modules are identifiable and
       which are not.


12.3.3 Extensions of algorithms for signal selection
       Several potential directions for extending the algorithms presented in
       Part III can be envisioned.

          • Incorporating the constraint that certain nodes cannot be excited or
            measured requires only minor modifications to our algorithms and is
            conceptually straightforward.

          • An economic setting where excitation and measurement at each node
            incur different costs. For example, all excitations may have a uniform
            cost of p B , and all measurements a cost of pC , with p B < pC .

          • If a set of excitations and measurements is already fixed, either pro-
            vided for free or previously deployed. The Pruning Search can readily
            accommodate such constraints.

       From a conceptual standpoint, these extensions appear relatively straight-
       forward and would likely require only minor adjustments to the existing
       algorithms. While some adaptation is needed, they do not seem to demand
       fundamentally new ideas or theoretical developments.
